Nevertheless, we found the Lower Bound Lemma necessary for study of
L^{2}
-integrability propagation, that allows for the considerably improvement and completeness properties of
L^{p}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
propagation norms proof, for
p\in(0,\infty]
, from the original argunent developed in
[
75
]
.

Lemma 8

(
Lower Bound Lemma).

Fix
\gamma\in(0,2]
, and assume
0\leq f:=f(\cdot,v)
satisfies

C\geq\max\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,dv,\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,|v|^{2}dv\right\}\geq\min\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,dv,\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,|v|^{2}dv\right\}\geq c>0\,,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,v\,dv=0\,,

for some positive constants
C
and
c
. Assume also that for some
\beta>0

0<\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\,|v|^{2+\beta}dv\leq B_{\beta}.

Then, there exists
c_{lb}:=c_{lb}(B,C,c,\beta,\gamma)>0
such that

\big(f\ast_{v}|\cdot|^{\gamma}\big)(v)\geq c_{lb}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\,.

(3.13)

with
0<c_{lb}
explicitly defined by

c_{lb}:=\frac{c}{4}\,\left(2\left(\frac{2^{2/\beta}}{c}\max\{C,B_{\beta}\}\langle\left(4C/c\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\rangle^{2+\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2-\gamma}}\left(1+\big(4C/c\big)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\right)^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}

(3.14)

Proof.

The case
\gamma=0
is trivial, thus, assume
\gamma\in(0,2]
. Take
r>0
,
v\in B(0,r)\subset{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, the open ball centered at the origin and radius
r
.
Note that for any
R>0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\{|v-w|\leq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2}&\text{d}w=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2}\text{d}w-\int_{\{|v-w|\geq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2}\text{d}w\\
&\geq c\,\langle v\rangle^{2}-\int_{\{|v-w|\geq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2}\text{d}w\\
&\geq c\,\langle v\rangle^{2}-\frac{1}{R^{\beta}}\int_{\{|v-w|\geq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2+\beta}\text{d}w\,.\end{split}

(3.15)

Since
|v|<r
, then

\int_{\{|v-w|\geq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2+\beta}\text{d}w\leq 2^{1+\beta}\max\{C,B_{\beta}\}\langle v\rangle^{2+\beta}\leq 2^{1+\beta}\max\{C,B_{\beta}\}\langle r\rangle^{2+\beta}.

Next, taking
R=R(r,c,C,\beta)
sufficiently large to satisfy

\frac{2^{1+\beta}}{R^{\beta}}\max\{C,B_{\beta}\}\langle r\rangle^{2+\beta}\leq\frac{c}{2}\,,

(3.16)

or, equivalently,

2\left(\frac{2^{2/\beta}}{c}\max\{C,B_{\beta}\}\langle r\rangle^{2+\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\leq{R}\,,

(3.17)

it follows from estimate (
3.15
) that

\int_{\{|v-w|\leq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2}\text{d}w\geq c\,\langle v\rangle^{2}-\frac{2^{1+\beta}}{R^{\beta}}\max\{C,B_{\beta}\}\langle r\rangle^{2+\beta}\geq\frac{c}{2}\,,\quad\forall\;v\in B(0,r)\,.

(3.18)

Hence, one can also infer from (
3.18
) that, for any
\gamma\in(0,2]
and
R
satisfying (
3.17
), it follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\cdot,w)|v-&w|^{\gamma}\text{d}w\geq\int_{\{|v-w|\leq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{\gamma}\text{d}w\\
&\geq\frac{1}{R^{2-\gamma}}\int_{\{|v-w|\leq R\}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{2}\text{d}w\geq\frac{c}{2R^{2-\gamma}}\,,\quad\forall\;v\in B(0,r)\,.\end{split}

(3.19)

Additionally, for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma\in(0,2]
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{\gamma}\text{d}w\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\cdot,w)\big(\tfrac{1}{2}|v|^{\gamma}-|w|^{\gamma}\big)\text{d}w\geq\big(\tfrac{c}{2}|v|^{\gamma}-C\big),\qquad\,.

(3.20)

As a consequence of (
3.19
) and (
3.20
),

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{\gamma}\text{d}w\geq\Big(\frac{c}{2R(r)^{2-\gamma}}\,\textbf{1}_{B(0,r)}(v)+\big(\tfrac{c}{2}|v|^{\gamma}-C\big)\,\textbf{1}_{B(0,r)^{c}}(v)\Big).

Choosing
r:=r_{*}=\big(4C/c)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\geq 1
one ensures that
\frac{c}{2}|v|^{\gamma}-C\geq\frac{c}{4}|v|^{\gamma}
for any
|v|\geq r
. Then, combining with the election of
R=R(r_{*})\geq 1
from (
3.17
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\cdot,w)|v-w|^{\gamma}\text{d}w\geq\Big(\frac{c}{2R(r_{*})^{2-\gamma}}\,\textbf{1}_{B(0,r_{*})}(v)+\frac{c}{4}\,|v|^{\gamma}\,\textbf{1}_{B(0,r_{*})^{c}}(v)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c}{4\,R^{2-\gamma}(r_{*})}\Big(\textbf{1}_{B(0,r_{*})}(v)+|v|^{\gamma}\,\textbf{1}_{B(0,r_{*})^{c}}(v)\Big)\geq\frac{c\,\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}}{4\,R^{2-\gamma}(r_{*})\langle r_{*}\rangle^{\gamma}}\,.

Thus, the lower bound (
3.13
) holds with
c_{lb}
as

c_{lb}=\frac{c}{4\,R^{2-\gamma}(r_{*})\langle r_{*}\rangle^{\gamma}}\,

Therefore, using that
r_{*}=\big(4C/c)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}
and invoking (
3.16
) to calculate
R(r_{*})
, the exact value of the lower bound constant
c_{lb}
representation (
3.14
) is obtained.
∎

Remark 4
.

This lower estimate is a functional inequality that control a convolution with a probability density from below.
In this context, the constant
c_{lb}
can be viewed as the analog to the Poincare constant fundamental to obtain the coerciveness property needed to develop an existence and uniqueness property in a suitable classical Sobolev space.

3.2.
moment-estimates for binary interactions

We start by proving the following identity, that can be found in
[
26
]
, giving a relation between local energies written in the scattering angle framework for center of mass and relative velocity coordinates from (
3.6
).

The following elementary estimate naturally follows from the binomial identity

Lemma 9
.

The following estimate holds

(z+y)^{q}-z^{q}-y^{q}\leq q2^{q-3}\big(z^{q-1}y+y^{q-1}z\big)\,,\qquad\text{for all}\quad 2\leq q\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.21)

Proof.

Note the identity

(z+y)^{q}-z^{q}-y^{q}=\int_{0}^{z}\int_{0}^{y}q(q-1)(t+\tau)^{q-2}\,d\tau\,dt\,.

For any
q\geq 2
, the function
|\cdot|^{q-2}
is convex, therefore,

(t+\tau)^{q-2}\leq 2^{q-3}\big(t^{q-2}+\tau^{q-2}\big)\,.

Thus, we obtain

(z+y)^{q}-z^{q}-y^{q}\leq 2^{q-3}\int_{0}^{z}\int_{0}^{y}q(q-1)\big(t^{q-2}+\tau^{q-2}\big)\,d\tau\,dt=q2^{(q-3)}\big(zy^{q-1}+z^{q-1}y\big)\,.

∎

In addition, in order to study of
sk
-moments of the solution to the Boltzmann equation with
0<s\leq 1
, it is also useful to invoke the following Lemma whose proof can be found in reference
[
32
]
, Lemma 2. The statement is transcribe here, adjusted to the current notation for the ease of the reader.

Lemma 10
.

Let the floor function associated to a real number, be denoted by
\lfloor q\rfloor:=\mbox{integer part of}\;q.
Then, for any
1<n\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in(0,1]
, let
1<sn\in\mathbb{R}
and
J_{sn}=\lfloor\frac{sn}{2}\rfloor
, the Newton’s generalized binomial expansion upper estimate for binomial forms with real valued exponents, is given by

\sum^{J_{sn}-1}_{j=1}{sn\choose j}\left(x^{sn-j}y^{j}+x^{j}y^{sn-j}\right)\leq(x+y)^{sn}-x^{sn}-y^{sn}\!\leq\!\sum^{J_{sn}}_{j=1}{sn\choose j}\left(x^{sn-j}y^{j}+x^{j}y^{sn-j}\right)

(3.22)

where the binomial coefficients for non-integer
sn
are
defined as

{sn\choose j}:=\frac{sn(sn-1)\dots(sn-j+1)}{j!},\ j\geq 1;\quad{sn\choose 0}:=1.

(3.23)

In addition, the case when
sn
is an odd integer the second inequality in (
3.22
)
becomes an equality, which coincides with the binomial expansion of
(x+y)^{sn}.

A natural consequence from the two inequalities in (
3.22
) are the following two estimates

\displaystyle\sum^{J_{sn}}_{j=0}{sn\choose j}\left(x^{sn-j}y^{j}+x^{j}y^{sn-j}\right)\leq K(x+y)^{sk},\qquad\text{for}\ 0<k\leq 2

and

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sum^{J_{sn}}_{j=1}{sn\choose j}\left(x^{sn-j}y^{j}+x^{j}y^{sn-j}\right)<\!2^{J_{sn}}\left(x^{sn-j}y^{j}+x^{j}y^{sn-j}\right).

Lemma
10
and its follow up estimates (
3.2
) provide a natural tool to study the
sn^{th}
-moments of collisional operator form
Q(f,f)(v,t)
describing a binary interaction framework, that arises
in the calculation of upper bounds terms from its positive contribution, as shown in the sequel.

The following energy identity is crucial for the development of moment estimates by means of the scattering angular averaging Theorem
12
. It is obtain writing the local energy conservation written for the Lebesgue weight functions in the post collisional velocities, that is
\langle v^{\prime}\rangle
and
\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle
by means of center of mass and relative velocity coordinates expressed in the scattering direction
\sigma
.

Lemma 11

(
Energy identity in the scattering direction coordinates).

For any
(v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{*})
and
(v,v_{*})
satisfying (
3.6
), let
E:=\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}
, and

\psi(x)
be any function defined in
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
,
then, the following identity holds

\begin{split}&\psi\big(\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}\big)+\psi\big(\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}\big)=\psi\bigg(E\Big(\tfrac{1-\xi\,\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\Big)\bigg)+\psi\bigg(E\Big(\tfrac{1+\xi\,\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\Big)\bigg)\,,\\
\vskip 6.0pt\cr&\ \text{ with }\ \ \ \xi=\frac{2|u|\,|V|}{E}\in[0,1]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hat{V}=\frac{V}{|V|}\,.\end{split}

(3.25)

Proof.

Setting the center of mass
V=\frac{v+v_{*}}{2}
and relative velocity
u=v-v_{*}
, we recall from (
3.6
) that

v^{\prime}=V-\frac{1}{2}|u|\sigma\quad\ \ \text{and }\quad v^{\prime}_{*}=V+\frac{1}{2}|u|\sigma\,.

As a consequence, we can compute as

\begin{split}\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}=1+|v^{\prime}|^{2}=1+\Big|V-\frac{1}{2}|u|\sigma\Big|^{2}&=1+|V|^{2}-|u|V\cdot\sigma+\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}=\frac{E}{2}-|u||V|\hat{V}\cdot\sigma\,,\\
\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}=1+|v^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}=1+\Big|V+\frac{1}{2}|u|\sigma\Big|^{2}&=1+|V|^{2}+|u|V\cdot\sigma+\frac{1}{4}|u|^{2}=\frac{E}{2}+|u||V|\hat{V}\cdot\sigma\,.\end{split}

Therefore,

\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}=E\bigg(\frac{1-\xi\,\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\bigg)\,,\qquad\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}=E\bigg(\frac{1+\xi\,\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\bigg)\,,\qquad\text{where}\quad\xi:=\frac{2\,|u||V|}{E}\,.

In addition, note that

0\leq\xi=\frac{2|u|\,|V|}{E}\leq\frac{|v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2}}{E}\leq 1\,,

which proves the lemma.
∎

3.3.
Angular averaging regularity

This is a profound result, independent of the solution of the Cauchy problem for the Boltzmann flow posed in Theorem
7
. It provides the basic component not only a coercive condition that yields global stability of solutions but also the control of high energy tails or exponential moments to be discussed in Subsection
5
.
It has its roots in the work of Povzner
[
79
]
, Desvillettes
[
43
]
and Wennberg
[
93
]
, but it was the ground breaking work of Bobylev
[
26
]
who introduced, by means of the energy identity (
3.25
), the concept of angular averaging that produces a broad and natural argument to control the integral of the sum of convex weight functions evaluated in post-collisional velocities proportional to the angular transition function
{\bf b}(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, without any need to omit point of singularities. While the original work in
[
26
]
was developed for the case of hard potentials an three dimensions (i.e.
\gamma=1
and
{\bf b}(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
being constant), we
present in this review
ideas techniques that are slight modifications of those from
[
32
,
51
,
4
]
. These new angular averaging approaches have been recently developed for a system of multi-component gas mixtures
[
53
]
, and more recently for a broad class of cross sections and transition probabilities for the case of polyatomic gases
[
54
]
.

Theorem 12

(
Angular Averaging Lemma).

Assume that the collision cross-section splits into
B(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)=\Phi(|u|)\,b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
and let
\psi_{r}(x)=\langle x\rangle^{2r}
, with any real value
r
. Then, there exists a positive constant
\mu_{r}
, referred by the
r^{th}
-contractive factor, such that the positive component of the weight function (
3.6
) in the weak formulation of the Boltzmann equation satisfies

G^{+}_{\psi_{r}}(v,v_{*})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big(\psi_{r}(v^{\prime})+\psi_{r}(v^{\prime}_{*})\big)B(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\leq\mu_{r}\,\Phi(|u|)\,\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)^{r}

(3.26)

where

\mu_{r}:=\sup_{\{\hat{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\hat{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\leq\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,,\ \ r>1\,.

(3.27)

\mu_{1}=\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
, and
\mu_{r}\searrow 0
strictly as
1<r\nearrow\infty.

Furthermore, if
b\in L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, with
1<p\leq\infty
, the following estimate holds

\mu_{r}\leq 2^{(2p-1)/p}\|b\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|^{(p-1)/p}\bigg(\frac{p-1}{p(1+r)-1}\bigg)^{(p-1)/p}=\mathcal{O}(r^{-\frac{p-1}{p}})\,.

(3.28)

Remark 5
.

The factor
\mu_{r}
generates constants
0<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{r}<1
, for all
r>1
, for the weak form of Boltzmann flow when tested by the polynomial forms
\psi_{r}
. In particular, the main result from this Theorem
12
, is to show a priori global in time
L^{1}_{k}
estimates to unique solutions of the Boltzmann initial value problem, where factor
\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{r}>0
is associated to coerciveness for the dynamics of the Boltzmann equation in a suitable Banach space and norm. See Remark
13
for further comments in coerciveness.

Proof.

Using Lemma
11
and the homogeneity of
\psi_{k}
, it follows that the positive part of the weight function (
3.6
) is controlled by

\begin{split}G^{+}_{\psi_{k}}(v_{*},v)&=\Phi(|u|)\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big(\psi_{r}(v^{\prime})+\psi_{r}(v^{\prime}_{*})\big)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\\
&=\Phi(|u|)\,\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)^{r}\,\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\xi\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\xi\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\,.\end{split}

Noting that the derivative of the integral termr
\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\xi\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\xi\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\bigg)
containing
\hat{V}\cdot\sigma
are non-negative, as their factor
\pm\xi
is bounded by unity, then

\left|\tfrac{1-\xi\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\xi\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\leq\left|\tfrac{1-\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\,.

(3.29)

Therefore,

\displaystyle G^{+}_{\psi_{r}}(v_{*},v)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(|u|)\,\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)^{r}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma

(3.30)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{r}\,\Phi(|u|)\,\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)^{r}\,,\qquad r>1\,.

Thus, we define the
r^{th}
-contractive factor

\mu_{r}:=\sup_{\{\hat{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\hat{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,,\qquad\text{for any}\ r>1\,.

Clearly,

\mu_{1}=\sup_{\{\hat{V}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\hat{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|+\left|\tfrac{1+\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma=\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}

In addition, for all
r>1
, the function

\mu_{r}(\hat{V},\hat{u}):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|\tfrac{1-\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}+\left|\tfrac{1+\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\,,

is continuous in the vectors
\hat{V}
and
\hat{u}
. Such continuity can be easily verify by using polar coordinates with zenith
\hat{u}
. Moreover, the integrand (
3.30
)being smaller or equal than unity, with respect to the measure
\left(b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)d\sigma\right)
, is strictly decreasing for
r>1
up to a set of measure zero (namely at
\sigma=\{\pm\hat{V}\}
).
As a consequence
\mu_{r_{1}}(\hat{V},\hat{u})>\mu_{r_{2}}(\hat{V},\hat{u})
for any
r_{1}<r_{2}
. Thus, the

\sup_{(V,\hat{u})}\left(\mu_{r_{1}}(\hat{V},\hat{u})-\mu_{r_{2}}(\hat{V},\hat{u})\right)>0
, when taken over any unitary vector pairs
(\hat{V},\hat{u})\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, which, by continuity, implies
\mu_{r_{1}}-\mu_{r_{2}}>0
. Finally, monotonic convergence gives that
\mu_{r}\searrow 0
, with
\mu_{r}<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
, as soon as
r>1
.

In the case that the scattering kernel
b
is integrable in
L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
with
p>1
, estimate (
3.28
) follows by Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\mu_{r}\leq\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\tfrac{1\pm\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{r}

\displaystyle b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)d\sigma\leq\|b\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\tfrac{1\pm\hat{V}\cdot\sigma}{2}\right|^{p^{\prime}r}d\sigma\bigg)^{1/p^{\prime}}\leq 2\|b\|_{p}\left(\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|}{1+rp^{\prime}}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}

(3.31)

\displaystyle=2^{(2p-1)/p}

\displaystyle\|b\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|^{(p-1)/p}\bigg(\frac{p-1}{p-1+pr}\bigg)^{(p-1)/p}\!\!\!=\mathcal{O}\left((1+\frac{p}{p-1}r)^{-\frac{p-1}{p}}\right),

and

\displaystyle\mu_{r}\leq 4\|b\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|((1+r)^{-1}),\qquad\text{for }p\equiv\infty.\qquad\qquad

(3.32)

∎

Recall that, since the unit sphere is a bounded set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the
L^{p}
-integrability of the transition probability function
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, with any
p\in[1,\infty]
, is control from below from the
L^{1}
integrability norm, that is

\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\leq\|b\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}|\mathbb{S}^{d-2}|^{(p-1)/p}
.

3.4.
Moment inequalities for binary interactions

The sharp form of Povzner Lemma in Theoren
12
has a profound impact in the theory of existence and uniqueness of the Boltzmann equation in the space homogeneous case because it allows to propagate and generate statistical moments in the case of hard potentials.

Lemma 13

(
Angular Averaging for the Binary Weight Function ).

The Boltzmann weight function (
3.6
) associated to the weak form (
3.5
) for
\varphi(v)=\langle v\rangle^{sk}
for
1<k\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in(0,1]
, now denoted by
G_{sk}(v_{*},v)
, satisfies the following inequality for any
sk\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{sk}(v_{*},v)&=\int_{S^{d-1}}\Big(\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2sk}+\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2sk}-\langle v\rangle^{2sk}-\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sk}\Big)B(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\\
&\leq\Phi(|u|)\Big(\mu_{sk}\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)^{sk}-\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\left(\langle v\rangle^{2sk}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sk}\right)\Big)\\[3.0pt]
&\hskip-28.45274pt\leq\Phi(|u|)\Big(2^{J_{2sk}}\mu_{sk}\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2}\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2(sk-1)}+\langle v\rangle^{2(sk-1)}\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)-(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{sk})\Big(\langle v\rangle^{2sk}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sk}\Big)\Big)\,.\end{split}

(3.33)

Proof.

It follows by a trivial application, first, of Theorem
12
and, then, of Lemma
10
with
x=\langle v\rangle^{2}
and
y=\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}
and
J_{2sk}=sk-1
.
∎

3.5.
Moment estimates - Version
\mathrm{I}

In order to obtain moment’s estimates associated to the solution of the Boltzmann equation that will also serve to calculate the summability of moments as a function of the potential rate
\gamma
from (
3.10
),
defined the
sk^{th}
-moment of the probability density
f(v,t)
by
m_{sk}[f](t)=\int f(v,t)\langle v\rangle^{2sk}dv,
as defined in (
3.2
), for any
sk\geq 0
, with
0<s\leq 1.

Lemma 14

(
Lebesgue moment’s estimates).

Each
sk^{th}
moment of the Boltzmann equation associated to the Cauchy problem Theorem
7

satisfies the following Ordinary Differential Inequality (ODI)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}m_{sk}[f](t)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{sk-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\mu_{sk}C_{\Phi}\Big(m_{1+\gamma/2}[f](t)m_{sk-1}[f](t)+m_{1}[f](t)m_{sk-1+\gamma/2}[f](t))\Big)

(3.34)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,m_{sk}[f](t)m_{\gamma/2}[f](t)-2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}c_{\Phi}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{sk})m_{0}[f](t)m_{sk+\gamma/2}[f](t),

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \text{for any }0<s\leq 1,\ 0<\gamma\leq 2,\ \text{and}\ sk>s{\bf k_{c}}\geq 1,\

with the
sk^{th}
contracting factor
\mu_{sk}<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
, and the lower bound constant
2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}c_{\Phi}
from the intermolecular potential function
\Phi(u)
.

Proof.

A direct application of Theorem
12
and Lemma
9
leading to Lemma
13
, enable estimates for any
sk^{th}
-moments of the collisional form
Q(f,f)(v,t)
, for any real valued
sk\geq 1
, as defined in (
3.2
), that is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}m_{k}[f](t)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}&Q(f,f)(v,t)\,\langle v\rangle^{2sk}dv=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}f(v)f(v_{*})\,G_{sk}(v_{*},v)\,dv_{*}dv\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}f(v)f(v_{*})\,\left(G^{+}_{sk}(v_{*},v)-G^{-}_{sk}(v_{*},v)\right)\,dv_{*}dv\\
&\quad\leq 2^{sk-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}f(v)f(v_{*})\left(\langle v\rangle^{2}\,\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2(sk-1)}+\langle v\rangle^{2(sk-1)}\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\right)\Phi(|u|)dv_{*}dv\\
&\qquad-(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{sk})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v)\langle v\rangle^{2sk}\Big(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v_{*})\Phi(|v-v_{*}|)dv_{*}\Big)dv\,.\end{split}

(3.35)

Thus, the first binary integral term, or positive part of (
3.35
) is integrated by Fubinni with respect to
v
and
v_{*}
, which combined with the upper of
\Phi_{\gamma}
from (
3.12
) yields the following upper bound given by the bilinear forms of shifted moments

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}f(v,t)f(v_{*},t)G^{+}_{sk}(v_{*},v)dv_{*}dv\!\leq\!2^{sk-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk}\Big(\!&m_{1+\gamma/2}[f]m_{sk-1}[f]\!+\!m_{1}[f]m_{sk-1+\gamma/2}[f]\!\Big)(t).\end{split}

(3.36)

The negative part of (
3.35
),
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}f(v,t)f(v_{*},t)G^{-}_{k}(v_{*},v)dv_{*}dv
, needs a lower lower bound for the collision frequency factor that uses the pointwise bound estimates for
\Phi_{\gamma}

from (
3.12
), in terms of the Lebesgue weights,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v_{*})\Phi_{\gamma}(|u|)dv_{*}

\displaystyle=m_{0}[f],\qquad\text{ for}\ \gamma=0\ \text{(Maxwell type interactions case)}\,,

and

(3.37)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v_{*})\Phi_{\gamma}(|u|)dv_{*}

\displaystyle\geq c_{\Phi}(2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}m_{0}[f]\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}-m_{\gamma/2}[f])\,,\qquad\text{ for}\ 0<\gamma\leq 2\ \text{(Hard potentials case)}\,,

Combining both estimates for the positive and negative parts of the collisional integrals estimates (
3.36
) and
(
3.5
) yields an upper estimate for the right hand side the
k^{th}
-moment ODI derived from the corresponding moments inequality (
3.35
), and so (
3.34
) holds.
∎

Remark 6
.

Note that the bound from below of
\Phi_{\gamma}
introduces a positive contribution, and so coarsening the negative contribution of the lower bound. However, this lower bound constants does not need any information about the solutions moment’s order.

Remark 7
.

Note that the third term positive term in the righthand side of (
3.34
) disappears for the case Maxwell interactions when
\gamma=0
.

It is important to point out that the above Ordinary Differential inequality (
3.34
) enables global in time propagation and generation of moments estimates, that are sufficient to prove the existence and uniqueness theory associated to the initial value problem in the proof of Theorem
7
in a suitable subspace of the positive cone of
L^{1}_{2k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
spaces. A minor and yet profound impact of the lower bound contribution from (
3.5
), is that requires to revisit the classical
k^{th}
-moment inequalities calculated with weights of the form
|v|^{2k}
rather the Lebesgue weight
\langle v\rangle^{2k}
.
Because of this “wrinkle” in the lower bound, we need to appeal to a
the following classical interpolation results for weighted moments associated to probability densities.

Lemma 15

(
Moment interpolation inequalities).

The moment
m_{j}:=m_{j}[f]:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v,t)\,\psi_{j}(v)\,dv
, for either
\psi_{j}(v)=|v|^{j}
or
\langle v\rangle^{j}
, satisfies

m_{j}\leq m_{{j}_{1}}^{\tau}m_{{j}_{2}}^{1-\tau},

(3.38)

where the positive constants
{j},{j}_{1},{j}_{2},\tau
satisfy
0<{j}_{1}<{j}<{j}_{2},

0<\tau<1
, and
{j}=\tau{j}_{1}+(1-\tau){j}_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof of this statement is straightforward since
\psi_{j}(v)
is convex for
j\geq 1
. Indeed, Hölder’s and Jensen’s inequality imply

\displaystyle m_{j}[f]\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(v)^{j}f(v)dv\

\displaystyle\leq\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(v)^{{j}_{1}\tau+{j}_{2}(1-\tau)}f(v)dv

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(v)^{{j}_{1}}f(v)dv\right)^{\tau}\,\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(v)^{{j}_{2}}f(v)dv\right)^{1-\tau}=m_{{j}_{1}}^{\tau}[f]\,m_{{j}_{2}}^{1-\tau}[f]\,.

∎

3.6.
Moment estimates for the collision operator in
L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

This task focus in obtaining Lebesgue moments estimates for the collisional integral
Q_{\gamma}(f,f)](v)
associated to the Boltzmann flow, by estimating its
\ell^{th}
-order Lebesgue weighted integrability in probability space
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\ell
a real valued number. That is, to estimate
m_{k}[Q(f,f)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q(f,f)(v)\langle v\rangle^{2k}dv
independently of time.
This is a delicate point as the goal is not only to find bound depending on
q
and the problem data, but also give an rather acquire description of the dependance of this bound with respect to the coerciveness factor
\mu_{r}
obtained by the Angular Averaging Lemma
12
.
Previous results along these pages from Subsections
3.4
and
3.5
, namely Lemma
13
on Angular Averaging for the Binary Weight Function, Lemma
14
on Lebesgue moment’s estimates, Lemma
15
on Moment interpolation inequalities and moments lower bounds (
3.5
), are the starting points of the following Theorem
16
and its proof developing the refined bounds.

Theorem 16

(
Lebesgue Moments of the collisional integral for hard potentials).

Let
f(v)\in L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
Q_{\gamma}(f,f)](v)
to the Boltzmann collision operator as posed in (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), with the with the collision kernel or transition probability
B(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)=\Phi(|u|)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, with
\Phi(u)
satisfying conditions (
3.10
), (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) for
\gamma\in(0,2]
and the angular transition part
b
integrable in
L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}),\ p\in[1,\infty)
.

Set
\ell=2sk
, for
s\in(0,1]
and integer
k
, to define the parameter

0<c:=c_{\gamma}(sk):=\frac{\gamma/2}{sk-1}

(3.39)

then, for any reference function
f_{0}(v)\in L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and any other
f(v)
satisfying
m_{i}[f]=m_{i\frac{\ell}{2}}[f]=m_{i\frac{\ell}{2}}[f_{0}]
,
i=0,2
, the
sk^{th}
-Lebesgue moment of the Boltzmann Collision operator is majorized by

\displaystyle m_{sk}[Q_{\gamma}(f,f)]

\displaystyle=\|Q_{\gamma}(f,f)\|_{L^{1}_{2sk}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q_{\gamma}(f,f)(v,t)\langle v\rangle^{2sk}dv

(3.40)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}(m_{sk}[f]):={\mathcal{B}}_{sk}\ -\ \frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}\,{m^{-c}_{1}[f_{0}]}m^{1+c}_{sk}[f],\ \ \text{for any }\ \ k\geq{\bf k_{c}}>1,\text{and}\ sk\geq s{\bf k_{c}}>1.

The constants
A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}
and
B_{k}
are determined solely from the Cauchy problem data associated for the Boltzmann flow stated in Theorem
7
and the order of
k
of the Lebesgue polynomial moment. They are

\displaystyle A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}=c_{\Phi}2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\,(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}})\,{m_{0}[f_{0}]}\quad\text{the coercive constant, }

and

(3.41)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}:=C_{\Phi}\left({2^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\mu_{{sk}}+2\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\right)\mathcal{C}_{sk}:=\beta_{sk}\,\mathcal{C}_{sk}\quad\text{the upper bound constant,}

both depending on the constant
k^{th}
-contracting factor
\mu_{{sk}}\in(0,1)
calculated in the Angular Averaging Lemma
12
, and the data associated to the Cauchy Problem posed in Theorem
7
.

In particular, there exists a rate
{\bf k_{b}}={\bf k_{b}}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})},m_{0}[f_{0}])
, such that for any
k\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}:=\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mn}}}:=\min\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1,

the quotient

\displaystyle\delta_{sk}\

\displaystyle:=\ \frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{\beta_{sk}}\leq\frac{\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,m_{0}[f_{0}]}{2^{sk+\gamma}\mu_{sk}+2^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}}<1.

(3.42)

Hence, the constant
\mathcal{C}_{sk}
estimated by the moments interpolation parameters is estimated by above by the constant
\mathcal{C}^{\text{up}}_{sk}
, related as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{sk}:={\tt\bf m}_{1,k}[f_{0}]\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})={\tt\bf m}_{1,k}[f_{0}]\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{\bf k_{c}})

(3.43)

with

\displaystyle R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})

\displaystyle:=R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{\bf k_{c}}):=\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{e_{\alpha}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{e_{\theta}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right],\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}\ \ e_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{c_{\gamma}(sk)},\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\ e_{\theta}=\left({sk}-1\right)\left(1+c_{\gamma}(sk)\right)^{2}+c_{\gamma}(sk),\quad\text{for }\ s{\bf k_{\gamma}}:=\frac{1+(\gamma/2)^{2}}{1-\gamma/2}>1,

(3.44)

for the factor
{\bf k_{\gamma}}
depending only on the potential rate
\gamma
and on fixed moment order
sk\geq s\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}=s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1
, with
{\bf k_{b}}
depending on
m_{0}[f_{0}]
and
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
.

The factor
{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}[f_{0}]
depends order of moment
k
and on data, namely, the invariant moments
m_{0}[f_{0}]
and
m_{1}[f_{0}]
and
\gamma
,

\displaystyle 0<{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}[f_{0}]:=\max\left\{m_{1}^{2c+1}[f_{0}]\,;\,m^{{sk}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}_{1}[f_{0}]m^{(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})\left(1+c\right)}_{0}[f_{0}]\right\}

(3.45)

Finally, combining (
16
), (
3.42
) and (
3.43
), set
{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}:=\beta_{sk}\,\mathcal{C}_{sk}
, enables the definition of the unique equilibrium state associated to the operator
\mathcal{L}(m_{sk}[f])(t)
from (
3.40
) defined as upper bound of the
k^{\vartheta}
-Lebesgue moment of the collisional integral as to be the unique root of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{E}_{k,\gamma}[f])

\displaystyle=0,

(3.46)

with
\mathbb{E}_{k,\gamma}[f]
is the unique equilibrium state, fully characterized by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{k,\gamma}

\displaystyle:=m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}[f_{0}]\left(\frac{{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}}{A_{\bf k_{c}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}=m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}[f_{0}]\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}{\mathcal{C}}_{sk}}{A_{\bf k_{c}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}

\displaystyle\,=\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{sk-1}{sk-1+\gamma/2}}\![f_{0}]\,:=\ R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\ :=\,R_{\mathbb{E}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{\bf k_{c}})

(3.47)

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})

\displaystyle:=\,R_{\mathbb{E}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{\bf k_{c}}):=\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\varepsilon_{\alpha}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right],

after following the analog notation from (
16
).

The rate factors
R_{\mathcal{C}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})
and
R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})
defined in (
16
) and (
16
), respectively, depend on mass and energy conserved quantities
m_{0}[f]
and
m_{1}[f]
, the integrable angular transition
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, potential rate
\gamma
, and Lebesgue moment order
sk
.

Remark 8
.

The statement of Theorem
16
replaces the definitions of
A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}
and
B_{k}
in (
16
), to
A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}=c_{lb}\,(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}})\,{m_{0}[f_{0}]}
and

{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}:=C_{\Phi}{2^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\mu_{sk}\,\mathcal{C}_{sk}:=\beta_{sk}\,\mathcal{C}_{sk},
respectively, and so
the new
\beta_{sk}:=C_{\Phi}{2^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\mu_{sk}.

Proof of Theorem
16
.

First note that if
f(v)\in L^{1}_{2k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, for
k>1
,
m_{1}[f]=m_{1}[f_{0}]
, then
f(v)\in L^{1}_{2sk}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
as
s\in(0,1]
. Equivalently, for
f
non-negative,
f(v)\in L^{1}_{2sk}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=m_{sk}[f]
, and so
m_{sk}[f]
is finite. In addition we assume that
f
is positive, non-singular measure, so
0<m_{0}[f]<m_{1}[f]
.

The
sk^{th}
-Lebesgue moment of the collisional integral was already estimated
in the right hand side the corresponding moment recursion Ordinary Differential Inequalities (
3.35
,
3.34
) and by below in (
3.5
) for any fixed
k
, such that
0<s\leq 1<sk<\infty
, after making use of the Angular Averaging
Lemma
12
, where the contractive constant
\mu_{sk}
was introduced.

The proof consists in estimating just the moment of the collisional integral appearing in the right hand side of the time dependent equations (
3.34
). These bounds are obtained by means of
interpolation estimates from (
3.38
), for any
\gamma\in(0,2]
and
{{\bf k_{c}}}>1
, that control all its positive terms bounded by above by the higher order moment
m_{k+\gamma/2}[f](t)
,
while controlling the negative contributions from below by coercive estimates proportional to
m_{k}^{1+\gamma/(2(sk-1)}[f]
, both multiplied by constants that only on the moment order
{{\bf k_{c}}}>1
and its corresponding
{{\bf k_{c}}}^{th}
-contracting factor
\mu_{{\bf k_{c}}}
, as well as on the mass and energy
m_{0}[f]=m_{0}[f_{0}]
,
m_{1}[f]=m_{1}[f_{0}]
, where
f(v)
is fixed.

Hence, these collision operator moment estimates are obtained from setting the non-linear factor

\displaystyle 0<c\ =\ c_{\gamma}(sk)\ :=\ \frac{\gamma}{2(sk-1)},\qquad\text{with}\ \gamma\in(0,2]\ \text{and}\ sk\geq{\bf s}{\bf k_{c}}>1\geq s>0,

(3.48)

that controls the maximum super linear growth to be determined by a lower bound of the moments of the loss operator.
Thus, while a Jensen’s inequality is enough to control part of it, the use of interpolations enables a good control for all the terms, since while mower moment of
f
are associated to the conservation properties of the Boltzmann flow, all other moments above
sk>1
will have estimates that are easily obtained from interpolation arguments.

Therefore, starting from the following interpolation, using the short
c
notation for
c_{\gamma}(sk)
,

\displaystyle m_{1+\gamma/2}[f]

\displaystyle\leq m^{\frac{1}{1+c}}_{1}[f_{0}]\,m^{\frac{c}{1+c}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\,,\qquad\ \ \ \ \ \text{and, }

(3.49)

\displaystyle m_{sk-1}[f]

\displaystyle\leq m^{\frac{s+\gamma/2}{sk+\gamma/2}}_{0}[f_{0}]\,m^{\frac{sk-1}{sk+\gamma/2}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]=m^{\frac{s+\gamma/2}{sk+\gamma/2}}_{0}[f_{0}]\,m^{\frac{1}{sk/(sk-1)+c}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\,.

With the first term in the right side of the inequality (
3.34
) is controlled as

\displaystyle m_{s+\gamma/2}[f]m_{sk-1}[f]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,k}[f_{0}]\,m^{\theta_{k}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\,,

with

(3.50)

\displaystyle C_{1,k}[f_{0}]=C_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\!:=\!m^{\frac{1}{1+c}}_{1}\![f_{0}]m^{\frac{s+\gamma/2}{sk+\gamma/2}}_{0}\![f_{0}],\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\theta_{k}\!:=\!\frac{c}{1\!+\!c}\!+\!\frac{1}{\frac{sk}{(sk-1)}\!+\!c}\!=\!\frac{\gamma/2}{\gamma/2\!+\!sk\!-\!1}\!+\!\frac{sk\!-\!1}{sk\!+\!\gamma/2}\!<\!1.

Furthermore,
m_{\gamma/2}[f]\leq m_{1}[f]=m_{1}(f_{0})<\infty
and, interpolating again,

\displaystyle m_{1}[f_{0}]m_{sk-1+\gamma/2}[f]\leq m_{1}[f_{0}]\,m_{sk}[f]\leq C_{2,sk}[f_{0}]\,m^{\alpha_{k}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\,,

\displaystyle\text{with}\ \ {C_{2,sk}[f_{0}]=C_{2,sk}[f_{0}]:=m^{\frac{c}{1+c}}_{1}[f_{0}]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \alpha_{k}:=\frac{1}{1+c}<1}\,.

(3.51)

Furthermore, both
\{m_{\gamma/2}[f]\,;\,\mu_{s}[f]\}\leq m_{1}[f]=m_{1}(f_{0})<\infty
, interpolating again

\displaystyle m_{sk}

\displaystyle<m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}\,m_{sk+\gamma/2}^{\frac{1}{1+c}}

(3.52)

then,

\displaystyle m_{1}[f_{0}]m_{sk-1+\gamma/2}[f]\leq m_{1}[f_{0}]\,m_{sk}[f]\leq C_{2,k}[f_{0}]\,m^{\alpha_{k}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\,,

\displaystyle\text{with}\ \ {C_{2,sk}[f_{0}]=C_{2,sk}[f_{0}]:=m^{\frac{2c+1}{1+c}}_{1}[f_{0}]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \alpha_{k}:=\frac{1}{1+c}<1}\,.

(3.53)

Therefore, from (
3.6
), (
3.6
), and the right hand side moment inequality (
3.34
), the following estimate hold

\displaystyle m_{sk}[Q(f,f)]

\displaystyle\leq\left(C_{\Phi}2^{(sk-1)(1+c_{\gamma}(sk))}\mu_{sk}+\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\right)\left[C_{1,k}[f_{0}]\,m^{\theta_{k}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]+C_{2,k}[f_{0}]\,m^{\alpha_{k}}_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-c_{\Phi}2^{-\gamma/2}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}})\,m_{0}(f_{0})\,m_{sk+\gamma/2}[f],

(3.54)

valid for any
\gamma\in(0,2]
,
0<s\leq 1
, and
k\geq{\bf k_{c}}>1.

Note that these
sk^{th}
-moment estimates provide a
coercive factor
independent of
k
, defined by

\displaystyle A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}:=c_{\Phi}2^{-\gamma/2}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}})\,m_{0}(f_{0}),\qquad\text{for }\ sk>s{\bf k_{c}}>1\geq s>0.

(3.55)

Next, the following key point enables the control of the right hand side of inequality (
3.6
) by proving that, after the use of a further interpolation, it is possible to obtain an upper bound for the positive contribution only depending on data and
k
.

For such task, take the couple of strictly less that unity exponents
\theta_{k}
and
\alpha_{k}
from (
3.6
) and (
3.6
), respectively, as well as constants
C_{1,k}[f_{0}]
and
C_{2,k}[f_{0}]
defined in (
3.6
) and (
3.6
), respectively, and invoke the
\delta
-Young’s inequality, namely,
|a\,b|\leq\delta^{-p/q}p^{-1}|a|^{p}+\delta q^{-1}|b|^{q}
, for
\delta>0
to be chosen sufficiently small, and
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
, making the following choice, for
c=c_{\gamma}(sk)
and
\theta_{k}
from (
3.48
) and (
3.6
), respectively,

\displaystyle a=C_{1,k}[f_{0}],\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ b=m^{\theta_{k}}_{k+\gamma/2}[f]\,,

with

\displaystyle p_{1}=\frac{1}{1-\theta_{k}}>1,\ \text{and its convex conjugate}\ \ q_{1}=\frac{1}{\theta_{k}}>1,

to obtain, again using the short
c
notation for
c_{\gamma}(sk)
,

\displaystyle C_{1,k}[f_{0}]\,m^{\theta_{k}}_{k}[f]\leq\delta^{-\frac{\theta_{k}}{1-\theta_{k}}}C^{\frac{1}{1-\theta_{k}}}_{1,k}[f_{0}]+\delta\,m_{k+\gamma/2}[f],

for

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\theta_{k}}=(1+c)(sk+(sk-1)c)=\left(1+c\right)\left(sk+{\gamma}/2\right),

(3.56)

and

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{k}}{1-\theta_{k}}=\left(sk+(sk-1)c\right)c+(1+c)(sk-1)+c=(sk-1)(1+c)^{2}+c,

which controls the first term of in (
3.6
).
A similar control for the second term of the positive contribution in (
3.6
), can be obtained choosing
c
and
\alpha_{k}
from (
3.48
) and (
3.6
), respectively, to yield

\displaystyle a=C_{2,k}[f_{0}],\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \ b=m^{\alpha_{k}}_{k+\gamma/2}[f],

with

\displaystyle p_{2}=\frac{1}{1-\alpha_{k}}=\frac{1+c}{c}>1,\ \text{and convex conjugate}\ \ q_{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{k}}=1+c>1,

to obtain
\frac{\alpha_{k}}{1-\alpha_{k}}=\frac{1}{c}=\frac{sk-1}{\gamma/2}
,

Hence, combining with (
3.6
),

\displaystyle C_{2,k}[f_{0}]\,m^{\alpha_{k}}_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f]

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-\frac{\alpha_{k}}{1-\alpha_{k}}}C^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha_{k}}}_{2,k}[f_{0}]+\delta\,m_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f]

(3.57)

\displaystyle=\delta^{-\frac{1}{c}}m_{1}^{\frac{c+1}{c}}[f_{0}]+\delta\,m_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f].

These previous calculations from moment interpolations enable the formulation os a new upper bound for
sk^{th}
-moments Inequalities (
3.6
), by estimating upper bounds of the maximum of estimates obtained in (
3.6
), and (
3.57
), respectively.

For the sake of simplicity of the calculations that follow, we set

\displaystyle\beta_{sk}:=2{C}_{\Phi}\left(2^{(sk-1)(1+c)}\mu_{sk}+\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\right)={C}_{\Phi}\left(2^{(sk+\frac{\gamma}{2})}\mu_{sk}+2\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\right).

(3.58)

The corresponding
k^{th}
-moments bounds of the collisional from (
3.6
) are majorized the the following Ordinary Differential Inequality

\displaystyle m_{k}[Q_{\gamma}(f,f)]

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{sk}C_{sk}\ -\ \Big(A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}-\frac{\beta_{sk}}{2}{\delta}\Big)\,m_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f]\,.

(3.59)

The next step consists in choosing a suitable
\delta=\delta_{sk}
, and associated constant
{C}_{sk}
to be defined below, that will preserve the coerciveness to secure a strict absorption effect from the higher order moment
m_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f]
. It is very important to notice that these choices only depend on the data, that are the potential rate
\gamma\in(0,2]
, the moment order for any fixed fixed
sk\geq s{\bf k_{c}}>1
, and the initial mass
m_{0}[f_{0}]
and energy
m_{1}[f_{0}]
, both time invariants by the Boltzmann flow.

Indeed, this is shown in the following Proposition.

Loss to Gain Moment Rate Property 1
.

There exist a large enough moment order
{\bf k_{b}}={\bf k_{b}}(m_{0}[f_{0}],b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma))
, such that time independent factor
\delta_{sk}
, defined for any
sk\geq{s{\bf k_{c}}}>1
, satisfies

\displaystyle 0<\delta:=\delta_{sk}\ =\frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{\beta_{sk}}\ <\ 1.

(3.60)

Proof.

This property is easy to prove since this choice is very natural, after evaluating the above quotient using (
3.55
) and (
3.58
), short labels for
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}
and
\beta_{sk}
, respectively. Indeed, the denominator in the above defenition of
\delta
, can be shown to grow as large as needed, but means of the angular averaging Lemma
12
. Indeed, from (
3.27
), (
3.31
) and (
3.32
),
the contracting factor
\mu_{sk}<\mu_{1}=\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
, so on one hand

\displaystyle 0<A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}=c_{\Phi}2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}})\,m_{0}[f_{0}]<c_{\Phi}2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,m_{0}[f_{0}].

(3.61)

On the other hand, from the definition (
3.27
) for
\mu_{sk}
after multiplying both sides by
2^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}
, yield the following estimate

\displaystyle\lim_{sk\to\infty}2^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\mu_{sk}\geq\lim_{sk\to\infty}\,2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bigg(\left|1-\hat{u}\cdot\sigma\right|^{sk}+\left|1+\hat{u}\cdot\sigma\right|^{sk}\bigg)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma=\infty\,

(3.62)

In particular, since data parameters from (
3.10
) assert
c_{\Phi}\leq C_{\Phi}
, there exists a

\displaystyle{\bf k_{b}}={\bf k_{b}}(m_{0}[f_{0}],b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)),

(3.63)

large enough such that, for all
{sk}\geq s\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}=:s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1
,

\displaystyle 0<\delta_{sk}\ =\frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{\beta_{sk}}\leq\frac{c_{\Phi}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,m_{0}[f_{0}]}{{C}_{\Phi}\left(2^{sk+\gamma}\mu_{{sk}}+2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+1}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\right)}<\frac{\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,m_{0}[f_{0}]}{2^{sk+\gamma}\mu_{sk}}<1.

(3.64)

∎

Finally, invoking the interpolation from (
3.52
),
yields the following lower bound for
m_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f]
,

\displaystyle\ m^{-c}_{1}[f_{0}]\,m_{sk}^{1+c}[f]\leq m_{{sk}+\gamma/2}[f]\,,

(3.65)

Which gives a bound from above to the righthand side in(
3.59
), with constants depending the moment order
k
, on the potential rate
\gamma
, the first two moments of the initial data
f_{0}
and the integrability properties of the angular function
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
. In particular, this estimate yields a super-linear negative term for the Lebesgue polynomial moment
m_{k}[f]
, namely

\displaystyle m_{k}[Q(f,f)]

\displaystyle<\beta_{sk}C_{sk}-\ \frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}\,{m^{-c}_{1}[f_{0}]}\,m^{1+c}_{sk}[f]\ =:\ \mathcal{L}_{k}(m_{k}[f])(t).

(3.66)

Hence, the estimates take the simple form
where the
k
-independent coercive factor
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}
, defined on (
3.55
), and the
sk
-dependent positive factor
\beta_{sk}\mathcal{C}_{sk}
takes the product the factor
\beta_{sk}
from (
3.58
), with
k\geq{\bf k_{b}}
, and

\mathcal{C}_{sk}
is characterized and estimated by above

\displaystyle C_{sk}=\max\Big\{\!\delta^{-\frac{\theta_{k}}{1-\theta_{k}}}\,{C^{\frac{1}{1-\theta_{k}}}_{1,{k}}[f_{0}]}\,,\,\delta_{sk}^{-\frac{\alpha_{k}}{1-\alpha_{k}}}\,{C^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha_{{k}}}}_{2,{k}}[f_{0}]}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{\!{C^{\frac{1}{1-\theta_{k}}}_{1,{k}}[f_{0}]};{C^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha_{k}}}_{2,{k}}[f_{0}]}\right\}\max\left\{\delta^{-\frac{\theta_{k}}{1-\theta_{k}}};\delta_{sk}^{-\frac{\alpha_{k}}{1-\alpha_{k}}}\right\}

\displaystyle={\tt\bf m}_{1,k}[f_{0}]\max\Big\{\delta_{sk}^{-[(k-1)(1+c)^{2}+c]}\,;\,\delta_{sk}^{-\frac{1}{c}}\Big\},\qquad\ \text{for any }sk>\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}={\bf s}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1,

(3.67)

with the factor
{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}[f_{0}]
being the one defined at (
3.45
), in the statement of this theorem.

To this end, the right hand of (
3.6
), can be further estimated by calculating
e_{\text{max}}
to be the exponent controlling both,
e_{\alpha}=e_{\alpha}(\gamma,sk)
and
e_{\theta}=e_{\theta}(\gamma,sk)
, for fixed
\gamma\in(0,2]
and
c=c_{\gamma}({sk})=\frac{\gamma/2}{sk-1}
, obtain by setting

\displaystyle e_{\text{max}}:=e_{\text{max},\gamma}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e_{\alpha}:=e_{\alpha}(\gamma,sk)={\frac{\alpha_{k}}{1-\alpha_{k}}}=\frac{1}{c},&\ \ \ \quad\text{for }sk\geq s{\bf k_{\gamma}}>1,\\[3.0pt]
e_{\theta}:=e_{\theta}(\gamma,sk)={\frac{\theta_{k}}{1-\theta_{k}}}={\left(sk-1\right)\left(1+c\right)^{2}+c},&\ \ \ \quad\text{for }1<sk<s{\bf k_{\gamma}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=:\left[e_{\alpha}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+e_{\theta}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right],

(3.68)

with a fixed
{\bf k_{\gamma}}
parameter characterized by how the order
k
compared to
{\bf k_{\gamma}}
, for any
0<\gamma\leq 2
. That means, the parameter
{\bf k_{\gamma}}
is exactly calculated from setting
{\bf c}=c_{\gamma}(s{\bf k_{\gamma}})=\frac{\gamma/2}{s{\bf k_{\gamma}}-1}
and finding the value for both exponents
e_{\theta}(k,\gamma)
and
e_{\alpha}(k,\gamma)
to coincide. That is,
s{\bf k_{\gamma}}
only depends on the hard potential rate exponent
\gamma\in(0,2]
, since

\displaystyle e_{\theta}

\displaystyle:=({s{\bf k_{\gamma}}}-1)(1+{\bf c})^{2}+{\bf c}=\frac{1}{\bf c}=:e_{\alpha}\ \ \iff\ \ \frac{\gamma}{2}=\frac{(s{\bf k_{\gamma}}-1)-\frac{\gamma}{2}}{(s{\bf k_{\gamma}}-1)+\frac{\gamma}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\ \iff\ \ \ s{\bf k_{\gamma}}=\frac{1+(\gamma/2)^{2}}{1-\gamma/2}>1.

(3.69)

As a consequence, taking
\delta_{sk}^{-1}:=\beta_{sk}\,A^{-1}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\ >\ 1
from (
3.60
), with
\beta_{sk}
from (
3.58
),
the second factor in (
3.6
) defines a coercive rate quotient

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{C}}(\delta_{k}^{-1})

\displaystyle:=\max\Big\{\delta_{sk}^{-[(k-1)(1+c)^{2}+c]}\,;\,\delta_{sk}^{-\frac{1}{c}}\Big\}

(3.70)

\displaystyle\equiv\left[\delta_{sk}^{-e_{\alpha}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+\delta_{sk}^{-e_{\theta}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]\equiv\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{e_{\alpha}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{e_{\theta}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]\equiv R_{\mathcal{C}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}).

It follows that the right hand side from
(
3.6
) is majorized by the upper estimate, now written in terms of the coercive rate quotient defined in (
3.70
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}

\displaystyle:=\beta_{sk}\mathcal{C}_{sk}:={\tt\bf m}_{1,{sk}}[f_{0}]\,\beta_{sk}R_{\mathcal{C}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}})

(3.71)

\displaystyle\equiv{\tt\bf m}_{1,{sk}}[f_{0}]\,\beta_{sk}\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{c}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\left({sk}-1\right)\left(1+c\right)^{2}+c}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right],

(3.72)

for any
{sk}\geq{s}\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}=s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}
, after gathering the exponents from (
3.6
) depending on
{\bf k_{\gamma}}
from (
3.6
).

The final upper bound for the
sk^{th}
-moment of the collision operator from (
3.66
) is controlled
by the following operator
\mathcal{L}_{sk}(m_{ks}[f])
, acting on the
sk^{th}
-Lebesgue moment of
f\in L^{1}_{2sk}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle m_{sk}[Q(f,f)]<\mathcal{L}_{sk}(m_{k}[f])=\beta_{sk}{\mathcal{C}}_{sk}-\ \frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}\,{m^{-c}_{1}[f_{0}]}\,m^{1+c}_{sk}[f],

(3.73)

whose unique root

\mathbb{E}_{k,\gamma}
is referred as to the equilibrium state, that is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}

\displaystyle:=m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}[f_{0}]\left(\frac{{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}}{A_{\bf k_{c}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}.

(3.74)

Written in terms of (
3.71
),
\mathbb{E}_{k,\gamma}
can be fully characterized by the data and
m_{0}[f]
and
m_{1}[f]
moments by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}

\displaystyle:=m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}[f_{0}]\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}{\mathcal{C}}_{sk}}{A_{\bf k_{c}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}\equiv m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}[f_{0}]R^{\frac{1}{1+c_{\gamma}(sk)}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}})

\displaystyle=\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{c}{c+1}}\!\![f_{0}]\,\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{1+e_{\alpha}}{1+c}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{k}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{1+e_{\theta}}{1+c}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]

\displaystyle\,=\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{sk-1}{sk-1+\gamma/2}}\![f_{0}]\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{k}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]

\displaystyle=:\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{sk-1}{sk-1+\gamma/2}}\![f_{0}]\,R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\,\quad\text{for any}\ \ sk\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},

(3.75)

after defining
R_{\mathbb{E}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{\bf k_{c}}):=R^{\frac{1}{1+c_{\gamma}(sk)}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}})\equiv R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})
. These new exponents obtained by taking
e_{\alpha}(sk)
and
e_{\theta}(sk)
defined in (
3.6
),
devided by
(1+c),
respectively, for
c=c_{\gamma}(sk)=\gamma/{(2(sk-1))}
, yields the simpler exponent associated to the the characterization
of
\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}
as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1+e_{\alpha}}{1+c}=\frac{1}{c}=\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1+e_{\theta}}{1+c}=sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}.

(3.76)

Thus, the proof on Theorem
16
is now complete.
∎

Remark 9
.

Note that, for example, in the classical case for
s=1
,
e_{\text{max}}=e_{\theta}
for
\gamma=2
and
e_{\text{max}}=e_{\alpha}
for values of
\gamma\approx 0^{+}
. In addition, in the case of hard spheres corresponding to
\gamma=1
, the exponent may change depending on the
k^{th}
-moment order, as
e_{\text{max}}=e_{\alpha}
for if
k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}=5/2
, but
e_{\text{max}}=e_{\theta}
if
k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}=5/2
.

In general, it follows that for any
sk>s\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1
, the constant
\mathcal{C}_{k}
, as characterized in (
3.6
), can be recasted by

\displaystyle{C}_{sk}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{C}_{sk}:={\tt\bf m}_{1,{sk}}[f_{0}]\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{c}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\left({sk}-1\right)\left(1+c\right)^{2}+c}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]

(3.77)

Remark 10
.

In the case where the initial mass
m_{0}[f_{0}]=1<m_{1}[f_{0}]
, after rescaling the Boltzmann solution by its initial mass, since
\delta=\delta_{sk}<1
, then for
sk>\max\{{\bf k_{\gamma}},{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1
by condition (
3.64
), the constant
\mathcal{C}_{sk}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{sk}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{c}}m_{1}^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}+(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})\left(1+c_{\gamma}(sk)\right)}[f_{0}],\quad\text{for any}\ \ sk\geq{s}\max\{{\bf k_{\gamma}},{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1\,.

(3.78)

Remark 11
.

This remark is fundamental to proof the existence of solutions to any moment either propagated or generated for as long the initial data is a non-negative
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2{\bf k_{c}}}
, with
\epsilon:=2({\bf k_{c}}-1)
, for
\epsilon\in(0,\gamma)
. In particular, setting the factor
s=1
, in the moments estimates,
the
sk^{th}
- moments of the collision operator remain valid when taking, for example
\mu_{k}<\mu_{\epsilon+1}<\mu_{1}=\|b\|_{L^{1}({\mathcal{S}}^{d-1})}=1
and
m_{0}[f_{0}](0)=1
.
With this choice, which suitable for the scalar binary elastic interacting Collisional form, whether both the initial mass
m_{0}[f_{0}]
and the angular transition function
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{1}({\mathcal{S}}^{d-1})
is renormalized to unit spherical average, naturally yields the order
{\bf k_{b}}
and
{\bf k_{\gamma}},
defined in (
3.63
) and (
3.6
), respectively, are larger than
{\bf k_{c}}=(2+\epsilon)/2.

This observation will be brought to readers’ attention in the proof of Lemma
23
, that extends the concept of solutions to any initial data
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}.

Remark 12
.

The estimates obtained in Theorem
16
for the moments of the collision operator are sufficient to prove the existence and uniqueness of the Boltzmann flow solutions in Section
4
, as posed in Theorem
7
on Section
3
, by means of Ordinary Differential Equations dynamics for time dependent flows in Banach spaces
L^{1}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

More specifically, Theorem
7
has two parts. The first one is to show existence and uniqueness in
L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
\ell>2+2\gamma
.
The second part is shown in Lemma
23
proving existence for lower order initial Lebesgue weights, that is in
L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
2+\varepsilon\leq\ell<2+2\gamma
.

3.7.
Moment Ordinary Differential Inequalities for Boltzmann flow solutions in
L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{+}

The following results derives a priori estimates for the Lebesgue polynomial moment time dynamics to solutions of the Boltzmann flow and rather accurate bounds as functions of the Cauchy Problem data. These estimates are, not only invoked in the second part of the of the proof of Theorem
7
as mentioned in the last Remark
12
, but also are critical for the moment summability properties that yield propagation and generation exponential moments globally in time with exponential rates fully characterized as a function of the coercive constant
A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}
from (
16
) and the Cauchy problem data as well. In addition these global moment estimates and bounds enable global in time propagation estimates for solutions in
L^{p}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\times R^{+}
, for
1\leq\ell\leq\infty
.

Theorem 17

(
Lebesgue Polynomial Moments
A priori
Estimates for hard potentials).

Let
f(v,t)\geq 0
be a solution of the Cauchy problem associated to the initial value problem for the Boltzmann flow in Theorem
7
, i.e. with collision operator
Q_{\gamma}(f,f)](v,t)
as defined in Theorem
16
.

Let
0<c:=c_{\gamma}(sk):=\frac{\gamma/2}{sk-1}
be the positive parameter defined in Theorem
16
, identity (
3.39
). Then, a super-solution to (
3.34
) is constructed by solving the following Ordinary Differential Inequality

\frac{d}{dt}m_{sk}[f](t)\leq{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}\ -\ \frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}\,{m^{-c}_{1}[f_{0}]}m^{1+c}_{sk}[f](t)\,,\qquad sk\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1,

\displaystyle=m_{sk}[f_{0}]\,\mathbb{I}_{m_{sk}[f_{0}]<\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}}\!

\displaystyle\qquad+\!\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{c}{c+1}}[f_{0}]\left[\left(\!\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\!\right)^{\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma}}\!\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\!\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\!\right)^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]\!\mathbb{I}_{m_{sk}[f_{0}]\geq\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}},

Proof.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}m_{sk}[f](t)\

\displaystyle<{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}-\frac{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}\,m^{-c}_{1}[f_{0}]\,m_{sk}^{1+c}[f](t)\,,\quad{sk}\geq\max\{s{\bf k_{b}},s{\bf k_{c}}\}>1,

(3.82)

where the

m_{sk}[f](t)
is finite for any positive time

Therefore, the goal is to construct to upper bound form of the right hand side the initial value problem associated to the Ordinary Differential Inequality (
3.82
), after setting
c=c_{\gamma}(sk)=\gamma/(2(sk-1))
once again,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&y^{\prime}(t)<{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}-A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,m_{1}^{-c}[f_{0}]y^{1+c}(t)\,,\quad\text{for}\ c(k)=\frac{\gamma/2}{sk-1},\quad t>0\,,\\
&y(0)=y_{0};\end{cases}

(3.83)

where

for

, and the corresponding value of initial data is
y_{0}
finite that may be any

finite with

In particular, for the case where

By classical ODEs comparison arguments (or the maximum principle in ODEs), it is easy to verify that any
y_{*}(t)
, the solution of the upper ODE problem to (
3.83
) with the initial data

, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&y_{*}^{\prime}(t)={\mathcal{B}}_{sk}-A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}\,m_{1}^{-c}y_{*}^{1+c}(t)\,,\qquad t>0\,,\\
&y_{*}(0)=y_{*,0},\end{cases}

(3.84)

controls the solution from above, i.e.

unifomly in time

.

(3.85)

is the unique
y_{*}^{\text{eq}}=\mathbb{E}_{c}
time independent equilibrium solution of

.

In addition, since the ODE (

, if the initial data

; while monotonically decreases to

, provided

.

Therefore, the propagation of the
k^{th}
-moment property follows easily from such comparison arguments between the solution of ODI (
3.83
), and the one for the ODE (
3.84
), yields the upper control to the solution of the initial value problem (

), with the following propagation of

, that is

y(t)\leq\max\Big\{y(0)\,;\,\mathbb{E}_{c}\Big\}\,,\\

or, equivalently, for the initial data

the global in time bound for each

-moment

\displaystyle m_{sk}[f](t)

\displaystyle\leq\max\Big\{m_{{sk}}[f_{0}]\,;\,m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}[f_{0}]\big(\tfrac{{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\big)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}\Big\}\,,\quad\text{for}\quad{sk}>1,\quad t>0,

(3.86)

with the coercive constant factor

and the upper bound constant

) of Theorem

.

The part of Theorem
17
’s proof concerning the
{sk}^{th}
-moments generation associated to the Boltzmann flow,
for any
sk
-order with only initial finite mass and energy and a moment of order
1+\varepsilon
, with
\varepsilon\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}-1.
This statement means that there is an instantaneous regularization, in the sense that it is possible to find an upper bound to solutions
m_{sk}[f](t)
, (
3.84
), or equivalently, (
3.83
) for

The following comparison arguments for generation of moment argument confronts the fact that the expected moment estimate at the initial time is actually defined by the unbounded
m_{sk}[f](0)=+\infty
, for
sk>s\max
. Yet, the following lemma remains true, as we describe the comparison argument.

Lemma 18
.

ODI’s Comparison Lemma for Moments Generation

Let
A,B
and
c
be positive constants and consider a function
y(t)
which is absolute continuous in
t\in(0,\infty)
and satisfies

y^{\prime}(t)\leq B-Ay^{1+c}(t)\,,\qquad\text{for}\ \ c>0,\quad t>0\,.

Then,

y(t)\leq\bar{y}(t):=E\bigg(1+\frac{K}{t^{\beta}}\bigg)\,,\qquad t>0\,,

for the choice

E=\mathbb{E}_{c}\bigg(\frac{B}{A}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}\,,\qquad\beta=\frac{1}{c}\,,\qquad K=\bigg(\frac{1}{c\,A}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{c}}\bigg(\frac{A}{B}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}\,.

Proof.

Note that in one hand

\bar{y}^{\prime}(t)=-\beta EKt^{-(\beta+1)}\,.

On the other hand,

B-A\,\bar{y}^{1+c}(t)=B-AE^{1+c}\bigg(1+\frac{K}{t^{\beta}}\bigg)^{1+c}\leq B-AE^{1+c}\bigg(1+\frac{K^{1+c}}{t^{\beta(1+c)}}\bigg)

where we invoked the binomial inequality
(1+\frac{K}{t^{\beta}})^{1+c}\geq 1+(\frac{K}{t^{\beta}})^{1+c}
. With the choice of
E,\,\beta
and
K
as suggested in the statement of the lemma it follows then

B-A\,\bar{y}^{1+c}(t)\leq\bar{y}^{\prime}(t)\,,\qquad t>0\,.

(3.87)

Next, note that for any
\epsilon>0
, the translation
\bar{y}_{\epsilon}(t):=\bar{y}(t-\epsilon)
satisfies (
3.87
) for
t>\epsilon
due to the time invariance of the inequality. Moreover, since
y(t)
is absolute continuous in
t\in[\epsilon,\infty)
, there exists
\eta_{*}>0
such that
\bar{y}_{\epsilon}(\epsilon+\eta)\geq y(\epsilon+\eta)
for any
\eta\in(0,\eta_{*}]
. Consequently, we conclude the following setting for any
\epsilon>0
and
\eta\in(0,\eta_{*}]
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&y^{\prime}(t)\leq B-A\,y^{1+c}(t)\,,\qquad t>\epsilon+\eta\\
&\bar{y}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(t)\geq B-A\,\bar{y}_{\epsilon}^{1+c}(t)\,,\qquad t>\epsilon+\eta\,,\\
&+\infty>\bar{y}_{\epsilon}(\epsilon+\eta)\geq y(\epsilon+\eta)\,.\end{cases}

(3.88)

Consequently, a standard comparison in ode implies that
\bar{y}_{\epsilon}(t)\geq y(t)
in
t>\epsilon+\eta
valid for any
\epsilon>0
and
\eta\in(0,\eta_{*}]
. In this particular case this fact can be readily proved denoting

\Phi(t)=\frac{y^{1+c}(t)-\bar{y}_{\epsilon}^{1+c}(t)}{y(t)-\bar{y}_{\epsilon}(t)}\,.

Since
|x^{1+c}-y^{1+c}|\leq(1+c)\max\{x^{c},y^{c}\}|x-y|
, it follows that
\Phi(t)
is bounded by

\big|\Phi(t)|\leq(1+c)\max\big\{y^{c}(t)\,,\,\bar{y}^{c}_{\epsilon}(t)\big\}<+\infty\,.

Thus, for
w(t)=y(t)-\bar{y}_{\epsilon}(t)
it follows from (
3.88
) that

w^{\prime}(t)+A\,\Phi(t)\,w(t)\leq 0\,,\qquad w(\epsilon+\eta)\leq 0\,,\qquad t>\epsilon+\eta\,,

which implies that

w(t)\leq w(\epsilon+\eta)e^{-A\int^{t}_{\epsilon+\eta}\Phi(s){\rm d}s}\leq 0\,.

Now, sending first
\eta
to zero and then
\epsilon
to zero it follows that
\bar{y}(t)\geq y(t)
for any
t>0
which is the statement of the lemma.
∎

Recall that the moments of
f(v,t)
satisfy the inequality (
3.83
), specifically, setting
c=c_{\gamma}(sk)=\frac{\gamma}{2(sk-1)}

m_{sk}^{\prime}(t)\leq{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}-A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,m_{1}^{-c}[f_{0}]\,m_{sk}^{1+c}(t)\,,\quad\text{for any}\ \ sk\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1,\quad t>0\,.

Thus, applying Lemma
18
with

B={\mathcal{B}}_{sk}\,,\quad A=A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,m_{1}^{-c}[f_{0}]\,,\quad

it follows that

\displaystyle m_{sk}[f](t)\

\displaystyle\leq\ \bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\bigg)^{\frac{c}{1+c}}m_{1}^{\frac{1}{1+c}}[f_{0}]+m_{1}[f_{0}]\bigg(\frac{1}{c\,A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}\,t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{c}}

\displaystyle\leq\ \mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}+m_{1}[f_{0}]\bigg(\frac{1}{c\,A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}\,t}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{c}}\,.

(3.89)

That is,
m_{sk}[f](t)
is finite for any
t>0
and for all
{s}k\geq{s}\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1
fixed. In addition, it becomes uniformly bounded in time for say
t\in[1,\infty)
satisfying

\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(m_{k}[f](t)-\mathbb{E}_{c}\right)\leq m_{1}[f_{0}]\,\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{c\,A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}\,t}\right)^{\frac{1}{c}}=0\,,

(3.90)

since the fixed term
A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}>0
for any choice of
{{\bf k_{c}}}>1
, which is the coercive factor defined in (
16
).

The proof of Theorem
17
is completed after the classification of the
Moments bounds estimates in terms of the coercive constant
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}
.

After combining the definitions of the coercive constant factor
A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}
, and the upper bound constant
{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}
in (
3.71
), all depending on
{sk}\geq\max\{s{\bf k_{b}},s{\bf k_{c}}\}>1
, a more explicit expression for the upper bound generation emerges when specifying the equilibrium state
\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}(sk)}
as a function of
\beta_{sk}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{sk}
and the initial moments associated to
m_{0}[f_{0}]
and
m_{1}[f_{0}]
, as characterized in (
3.73
).
Therefore, the moment bounds estimates, focusing on the dependance of the coercive constant
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}
are recasted, after invoking the moment propagation estimates (
17
), as stated in Theorem
17
,

Moment’s propagation estimates:

\displaystyle m_{sk}[f](t)

\displaystyle\!\leq\!\max\!\left\{m_{sk}[f_{0}]\,;\,\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{ks-1}{sk-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}[f_{0}]\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{2(ks-1)}{\gamma}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{k}}{A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]\right\},

(3.91)

uniformly in
t>0,
for any
{s}k>{s}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}
. Analogously, the moments generation estimates (
3.81
) are characterized by
Moment’s generation estimates:

\displaystyle m_{sk}[f](t)

\displaystyle\leq\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}\right)^{\!\frac{ks-1}{sk-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}[f_{0}]\left[\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma}}\!\!\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{sk+\gamma/2}\!\!\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+m_{1}[f_{0}]\bigg(\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma\,A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,t}\bigg)^{\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma}}\,.

(3.92)

In addition, if
sk>\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1
, then
\beta_{sk}A^{-1}_{{\bf k_{c}}}>1.

Thus, using the notation (
16
) as defined earlier, one can write

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma}}\!\!\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{sk}}{A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{sk+\gamma/2}=R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})=R_{\mathbb{E}}(\beta_{sk}/A_{\bf k_{c}}).

(3.93)

The proof of Theorem
