∎

Remark 13
.

Coerciveness.

The control of the upper bound any
{sk}^{th}
-moment that solves the differential inequality (
3.55
), degenerates as the term
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}:=c_{\Phi}2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\|b\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{{\bf k_{c}}}){m_{0}[f_{0}]}
, defined in (
3.27
), would vanish. The derivation of this term
depends on the choice of of the positive term
0<\delta_{sk}<1
made in (
3.42
), for any
sk>s\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{\bf k_{c}}\}>1
. This choice depends on the decaying property of the contractive constant
\mu_{s}k
for any
{sk}\geq{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}>1
calculated in the Angular Averaging Lemma
12
, the pointwise factors for the potential from (
3.10
) and (
3.12
) depending on the data.
In addition, that the constant
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}
is calculated without using that
f
solves the Boltzmann equation, it only depends
on the spherical integrability (or angular averaging) of the transition probability
B(\Phi(|u|),\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},d\sigma)
, and lower constants
c_{\Phi}
and
C_{\Phi}
(
3.10
) and (
3.12
), associated to the the potential rate
\Phi(v).

That means
A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}
can be viewed as the coercive constant related to the Cauchy problem to Boltzmann flow for elastic interactions with transition probabilities corresponding to hard potentials with an integrable angular transition probability, posed in the Banach space with the natural
k
-Lebesgue weight norm.

Hence, the a priori
{sk}^{th}
-moment estimates obtained in Theorem
17
induced an upper uniform bound in time for each
{sk}
fixed with a coercivity property for any
{sk}\geq{s}\max\{{\bf k_{b}},{{\bf k_{c}}}\}>1
. One can view these a priori estimates as analog to significance of the Sobolev-Poincare and coercive constants associated to elliptic and parabolic equations in divergence form. The corresponding existence and uniqueness theorem in these Banach spaces follow in the next section.

We conclude this section by stating the corresponding
L^{1}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
or
K^{th}
-moment estimates for the Boltzmann flow with Maxwell type collisional forms corresponding to potential rates
\gamma=0
, corresponding to transition probability with potential rate
\gamma=0
and integrable angular factor
(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)

3.8.
Case for Maxwell type of interactions corresponding to
\gamma=0
.

This is the well known case where the estimate (
3.34
)
is obtained by a moment recursion formula for classical cases where the transition probability of collision kernel simple depend on an integrable
B(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},d\sigma)
.

However, the interpolation estimate techniques developed in
Theorem
16
and Theorem
17
extend to the case
c_{\Phi}b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\leq B(\Phi(|u|),\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\leq C_{\Phi}b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, with
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},d\sigma)
and
c_{\Phi}
and
C_{\Phi}
the bounds from (
3.10
) and (
3.12
), respectively.

Indeed,
similar estimates by above for the gain operator by above using interpolation, while an exact negative identity for the moments of the loss, which clearly maintains the linearity property of the moments estimates, to obtain the corresponding ODI

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}m_{k}[f](t)

\displaystyle=m_{k}[Q_{0}[f,f](t)\leq C_{\Phi}2^{k}\mu_{k}m_{1}[f_{0}]m_{k-1}[f]-c_{\Phi}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{k})m_{0}[f_{0}]m_{k}[f]

\displaystyle\leq K_{1,k}[f_{0}]m^{\frac{k-2}{k-1}}_{k}[f]-(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{k})m_{0}[f_{0}]m_{k}[f]

(3.94)

\displaystyle\leq K_{2,k}[f_{0}]-\tfrac{c_{\Phi}}{2}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{k})m_{0}[f_{0}]m_{k}[f]=K_{2,k}[f_{0}]-\frac{c_{\Phi}}{2}A_{\bf k_{c}}m_{0}[f_{0}]m_{k}[f],

\displaystyle=:\mathcal{L}^{M}_{k}(m_{k}[f]),\qquad\quad k\geq{\bf k_{c}}\geq 1\,,

where
\mathcal{L}^{M}_{k}(y)
is a linear operator in
y=m_{k}[f]
, with
constants
A_{\bf k_{c}}
the coercive one for
{\bf k_{c}}>1
, and
K_{i,k}[f_{0}]
, for
I=1,2
, depend on
C_{\Phi},\mu_{k}
the interpolation factors. This case can only fulfilled the moment propagation property of a given initial data, as the negative term in the
k^{th}
-moment ODI makes a linear upper ODE with an absorption term
m_{k}[f](t)
and with a bounded in time right hand side. Hence, this inequality leads to estimate (
17
) and the is no mechanism that can generate a moment or order
k
from just an initial data with bounded mass and energy. More precisely, for any
f_{0}(v)\in L^{1}_{2k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
the
k^{th}
Lebesgue moment associated to the Maxwell type of interactions Ordinary Differential Inequality (
3.8
) is global in time controlled by

\displaystyle m_{k}[f](t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{k}+m_{k}[f_{0}]\exp(-\frac{c_{\Phi}}{2}A_{\bf k_{c}}m_{0}[f_{0}]\,t),

(3.95)

\displaystyle\text{with}\qquad\mathbb{E}_{k}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2K_{2,k}}{c_{\Phi}A_{\bf k_{c}}m_{0}[f_{0}]}\ \text{being the unique root of linear operator}\ \mathcal{L}^{M}_{k}(m_{k}[f])\,

making
\mathbb{E}_{k}
the unique equilibrium state of an associated upper ODE
y^{\prime}(t)=\mathcal{L}^{M}_{k}(y(t))
.

In particular the existence theorem to be developed in the next sections applies to this case as well, although the classical theory of ODEs, for comparisons to linear theory, since the collision operator can be shown to be a Lipschitz form, and the existence and uniqueness of
f(v,t)
solving the Cauchy problem if obtained by a classical Picard type iteration constructs a fulfilling the global moments bounds (
3.95
). Much work has been written for the theory of conservative and non-conservative models for Maxwell type of interactions, and a sample of them can be found at
[
25
,
24
,
30
,
29
,
31
,
27
]
and references therein.

4.
Proof of existence and uniqueness Theorem
7

All estimates for
k^{th}
-Lebesgue polynomial moments for
1<k\in{\mathbb{R}}
, obtained in Theorem
16
, are enough to prove the existence and uniqueness of solutions to the initial value problem (
3.8
), for the space homogeneous problem of Theorem
7
for Maxwell and hard potentials for
\gamma\in[0,2]
and integrable angular scattering transition probability rate.
This result is based on the following result on Banach spaces, Theorem
19
, which was initially proposed in the unpublished note
[
34
]
in order to solve the Boltzmann equation for hard spheres in three dimensions, that is
\gamma=1
, and uniform scattering kernel. While the ideas to prove this theorem are somehow classical, see for example
[
71
]
, we revisit this approach here showing the use of the a-priori estimates on the
k^{th}
-Lebesgue polynomial moments and their impact to verify that the sub-tangent condition is satisfied, for any real valued
1<k\in\ {\mathbb{R}}
. The proof of the sub-tangent is crucial for the selection of the solvability invariant set. This point not covered in the proposed proof in
[
34
]
. The following argument fully completes the argument sub-tangent condition being a sufficient one. It also extends the argument for the aforementioned more general models for of collision cross sections for the homogeneous Boltzmann equation for elastic binary interactions, and other broader problems such collisional multi-species gas mixtures, as we well as polyatomic gas models.

Following the notation used earlier for the Lebesgue norm
\|g\|_{L^{1}_{\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
for
\ell\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and the
k^{th}
-moment
m_{k}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}g(v)\langle v\rangle^{2k}dv
, as defined in(
3.1
) and (
3.2
), respectively, notice that the norm and moments coincide for a positive
g(v)
and
2k=\ell
.

Hence, focusing the proof of initial value problem stated Theorem
7
, initial data is given by moments of positive probability density functions taking the identical notation
m_{0}:=m[f_{0}]=\|f_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
and
m_{1}:=m_{1}[f_{0}]=\|f_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
which are initial mass and energy invariants of the Boltzmann flow, respectively. In addition, since
k^{th}
-moments associated to to time dependent probability densities
f(v,t)
, the short notation
m_{k}(t):=m_{k}[f](t)
will be used, unless it is relevant for the the Boltzmann solution flow.

Theorem 19
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:=(\mathcal{E},\|\cdot\|)
be a Banach space,
\Omega
be a bounded, convex and closed subset of
\mathcal{E}
, and
Q:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be an operator satisfying the following properties:

i)

Hölder continuity condition

\big\|Q[f]-Q[g]\big\|\leq C\|f-g\|^{\beta},\quad\beta\in(0,1),\quad\forall\,f,g\in\Omega\,.

(4.1)

ii)

Sub-tangent condition

\liminf_{h\rightarrow 0^{+}}h^{-1}\text{Dist}\big(f+h\,Q[f],\,\Omega\big)=0,\qquad\forall\,f\in\Omega\,,

(4.2)

where
\text{Dist}(v,\Omega):=\inf_{\omega\in\Omega}\|v-w\|
.

iii)

One-sided Lipschitz condition

\big[Q[f]-Q[g],f-g\big]_{-}\ \leq C\ \|f-g\|,\qquad\forall\,f,g\in\Omega\,,

(4.3)

where
\big[\varphi,\phi\big]_{-}\ :=\ \lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{-}}h^{-1}\big(\|\phi+h\varphi\|-\|\phi\|\big)
.

Then, the abstract
ode
problem

\partial_{t}f=Q[f]\quad\text{on}\quad[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{E},\qquad f(0)=f_{0}\in\Omega,

has a unique solution in
\mathcal{C}([0,\infty),\Omega)\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}((0,\infty),\mathcal{E})
.

Remark 14
.

In the Banach space
L^{1}_{1}
, the one-sided Lipschitz bracket reduces to

\displaystyle\big[\varphi,\psi\big]_{-}\ =\ \lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{-}}h^{-1}\big(\|\varphi+h\psi\|_{L^{1}_{2}}-\|\varphi\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt=\lim_{h\rightarrow 0^{-}}\int h^{-1}\Big(\big|\varphi+h\psi\big|-|\varphi|\Big)\ \langle v\rangle^{2}\ dv=\ \int\psi\,\text{sign}(\varphi)\ \langle v\rangle^{2}\ dv\,.

The last step follows by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence because

\big|h^{-1}\big|\,\Big|\big|\varphi+h\psi\big|-|\varphi|\Big|\leq|\psi|\,,

and,

h^{-1}\Big(\big|\varphi+h\psi\big|-|\varphi|\Big)=\frac{2\,\varphi\,\psi+h\,\psi^{2}}{\big|\varphi+h\psi\big|+|\varphi|}\rightarrow\psi\,\text{sign}(\varphi)\quad\text{as}\quad h\rightarrow 0\,.

We point out that the one-sided Lipschitz condition (
4.3
) is sufficient to obtain uniqueness for the Boltzmann problem as noticed by
[
46
]
.

The proof of theorem
19
is shown in the Appendix, in the exact version as done by A. Bressan in the unpublished work of
[
34
]
. For completeness, it is included in his manuscript.

Proof.

The Cauchy problem as posed in Theorem
7
.

without loss of generality taking just
s=1
. This proof consists in two stages. The first stage shows that conditions
i), ii)
and
iii)
from Theorem
19
are fulfilled for the collision operator
Q:=Q_{\gamma}(f,f)(v)
as defined in the statement of Theorem
16
with all properties proved in that theorem, after setting Banach space
{\mathcal{E}}:=L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

and the subset
\Omega\subset L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
defined as follows.
Set
k=1+\gamma
be moment
m_{1+\gamma}[f]=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v)\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}dv
and consider the constant
\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}:=\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}+{\mathcal{B}}_{1+\gamma}
, where

\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}
is the only root of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y):={\mathcal{B}}_{k}-\frac{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}m^{-c_{\gamma}(k)}_{1}[f_{0}]y^{1+c_{\gamma}(k)}
as defined in (
3.40
).

Hence, define the bounded convex subset
\Omega\subset L^{1}_{2}({{\mathbb{R}}}^{d})
by

\begin{split}\Omega_{\gamma}:=&\Big\{\ g\in L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\ :\ g\geq 0,\int g(v)\,dv\!=\!m_{0},\int g(v)|v|^{2}dv\!=\!m_{1}-m_{0}>0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\int g(v)\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}dv=m_{1+\gamma}\!\leq\!K\!<\!\infty\ \Big\},\end{split}

(4.4)

for any finite constant
K>\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}.

In fact, conditions
i)
and
iii)
are satisfied with any initial moment to have at least
1+\gamma
moments, that is
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, while the Sub Tangent condition
ii)
proof enables the existence as well characterizes the bounded, convex, closed subset
\Omega\in\mathcal{E},
by that any element
g\in\Omega
may have moment
m_{1+\gamma}[g]\geq\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}.

The second stage consists in showing the solution set can be enlarge to solution set posing just initial data in
L^{1}_{2^{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by means of relaxing the initial data to the lower moment condition
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for some
\epsilon>0
. It covers two delicate points. The first one if to construct a sequence of approximants in the set
\Omega
to the initial data
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by density and, the second one consists in showing that the sequence of solutions is uniformly bounded strongly in
L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}
and so has a limit that generates the solution for such
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Remark 15
.

It is important to notice that the constant
\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}
is fully determined only by the transition probability, or collision kernel term
B_{\gamma}(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, and the initial conserved quantities
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
, and so it
only depends on the data associated to the Cauchy problem to solved in Theorem
7
.

Without loss of generality, the binary collisional Boltzmann operator
Q_{\gamma}(\cdot,\cdot)
if considered to have
B_{\gamma}(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma):=|u|^{\gamma}\,b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, with
\gamma\in[0,2]
, and the angular transition measure
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
is in
L^{1}({\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}).

The subindex
\gamma\in[0,2]
on
Q_{\gamma}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the potential rate. Thus,
Q_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)
represents the Maxwell Type of interactions.

This following Lemma shows the Hölder property
i)
hold for any
g\in L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\ell\geq 2(1+\gamma)
. It does not need the conservation property stating that any pair of functions must have the same
L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
\ell=1,2
, since
L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{1}_{2(1+\gamma)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

Lemma 20

(
Hölder estimate for elements
L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
\ell\geq 2(1+\gamma)
– Property i)).

Let
f
and
g
functions lying in
L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
\ell\geq 2(1+\gamma)
, with their
L^{1}_{2(1+\gamma)}
-norms are bounded. Denote by
h=f-g
and
H=f+g
, noticing that
H
is nonnegative whenever
f
and
g
are nonnegative.
Then,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big|Q_{\gamma}(f,f)-Q_{\gamma}(g,g)\big|\langle v\rangle^{2}\ dv\leq\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,C_{\Omega}\,\|f-g\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}}\,.

with constant given by
C_{\Omega}:=6\,K^{3/2}
.

Proof.

Observe that for
h=f-g
and
H=f+g
, the following identity holds

Q_{\gamma}(f,f)-Q_{\gamma}(g,g)\ =\ \tfrac{1}{2}Q_{\gamma}(H,h)+\tfrac{1}{2}Q_{\gamma}(h,H)\,.

In addition, note that

|u|=|u^{\prime}|\leq\min\big\{\langle v\rangle\langle v_{*}\rangle,\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle\big\}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\langle v\rangle\leq\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle\,,

where the latter uses local conservation of energy
|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|v^{\prime}_{*}|^{2}=|v|^{2}+|v_{*}|^{2}
.

Therefore,

\big|Q_{\gamma}(h,H)\big|\langle v\rangle^{2}\leq Q^{+}_{0}(|h|\langle v\rangle^{2+\gamma},H\langle v\rangle^{2+\gamma})+Q^{-}_{0}(|h|\langle v\rangle^{2+\gamma},H\langle v\rangle^{2+\gamma})\,.

Moreover, multiplying
Q^{\pm}_{0}(|\psi|,|\varphi|)(v)
by any
\psi,\varphi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, in
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following identity holds

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q^{\pm}_{0}(|\psi|,|\varphi|)(v)dv=\|b\|_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\|\psi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\varphi\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,

that allow us to conclude that
Q_{\gamma}(h,H)
is control by the
(1+\gamma/2)
-moments of
h
and
H
, respectively, proportionally to the integral of the angular transition function
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, that is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|Q_{\gamma}(h,H)\big|\langle v\rangle^{2}\ dv\leq 2\ \|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\ \|H\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}}\ \|h\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}}\,.

Further, using Lebesgue’s interpolation, which uses the
L^{1}_{2(1+\gamma)}
-norm of
H=f+g
, yields

\|h\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}}\ \leq\|h\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2(1+\gamma)}}\|h\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}}\leq\|H\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2(1+\gamma)}}\|h\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}}\,,

(4.5)

where the second inequality follows by observing that for nonnegative functions
|h|\leq H
. Therefore,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big|Q_{\gamma}(h,H)\big|\langle v\rangle^{2}\ dv\leq 2\ \|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\ \|H\|^{3/2}_{L^{1}_{2(1+\gamma)}}\ \|h\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}}\leq 6\,K^{3/2}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|h\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}}\,.

(4.6)

The latter estimate holds since
\|H\|_{L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}}\leq 2m_{1+\gamma}[f]\leq 2K
for any
f,g\in\Omega
.

∎

Lemma 21

(
Sub-tangent condition – Property ii)).

Fix
f\in\Omega
. Then, for any
\epsilon>0
there exists
\eta_{*}:=\eta_{*}(f,\epsilon)>0
such that the ball centered at
f+\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f)
with radius
\eta\,\epsilon>0
intersects
\Omega
. That is,

B(f+\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f),\eta\,\epsilon)\cap\Omega,\;\text{is non-empty for any}\;0<\eta\leq\eta_{*}\ .

(4.7)

In particular,

\lim_{\eta\to 0+}\eta^{-1}\text{Dist}\big(f+\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f),\Omega\big)=0\,.

(4.8)

Proof.

The proof consists, for a given
f(v)\in\Omega
, we construct an associated function, denoted by
w_{R,f}(v)
and first show that it is possible to find a small parameter
\eta(\epsilon,f)
, such that
w_{R,f}(v)\in\Omega
.
The second part simple shows that, for the given arbitrary
\epsilon>0
, also
w_{R,f}(v)\in B(f+\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f),\eta\,\epsilon)
.

Part 1:
Start by recalling
m_{0}:=m_{0}[f](0)
and
m_{1}:=m_{1}[f](0)
, the conserved in time quantities mass and mass plus energy, respectively for any distribution density
f(v,t)\in\Omega
.

Next, consider the angular transition
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
integrable and define the following cut off function depending on the fixed
f\in\Omega
with a couple of positive real valued parameters
R
and
\eta
, to be chosen later, given by

\displaystyle w_{R,f}(v)

\displaystyle:=f(v)+\eta\,Q(f_{R},f_{R})(v)

(4.9)

\displaystyle\ =f(v)+\eta\Big(Q^{+}_{\gamma}(f_{R},f_{R})(v)-f_{R}(v)\|b\|_{L^{1}({\mathcal{S}}^{d-1})}\big(f_{R}\ast\Phi\big)(v)\Big)\,,

with
f_{R}(v):=\text{1}_{\{|v|\leq R\}}\,f(v)\geq 0
, and the potential function
\Phi(v)
satisfies (
3.10
) and (
3.12
).

The arguments that follow prove that
w_{R,f}(v)
is an element in the set (
4.7
) for
R>0
, chosen sufficiently large, and
\eta>0
in (
4.8
) chosen sufficiently small, both parameters only depending on
\epsilon
and
f
.

Indeed, observing that
\gamma\in(0,2]
, and invoking (
3.12
), the collision frequency is bounded above by,

\displaystyle\big(f_{R}\ast\Phi\big)(v)\leq 2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}m_{0}[f](0)(1+|v|^{\gamma})=C(m_{0})(1+|v|^{\gamma})\,,

(4.10)

then, choosing
0<\eta\leq 1/\big(\|b\|_{L^{1}({\mathcal{S}}^{d-1})}C(m_{0})\big(1+R^{\gamma}\big)\big)
, the function
w_{R,f}(v)
can be estimated from below by

\displaystyle w_{R,f}(v)

\displaystyle\geq f(v)+\eta\,\Big(Q^{+}_{\gamma}(f_{R},f_{R})(v)-\text{1}_{\{|v|\leq R\}}\,f(v)\|b\|_{L^{1}({\mathcal{S}}^{d-1})}\big(f_{R}\ast\Phi\big)(v,t)\Big)

(4.11)

\displaystyle\geq f(v,t)\Big(1-\eta\|b\|_{L^{1}({\mathcal{S}}^{d-1})}C(m_{0})\big(1+R^{\gamma}\big)\Big)\geq 0\,,\qquad\text{for}\ C(m_{0}):=2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}m_{0}[f].

In addition, since the definition of
w_{R,f}(v,t)
in (
4.9
) preserves the binary structure of the collisional form

Q(f_{R},f_{R})(v)
, then by conservation of mass and energy, both such Lebesgue moments of
w_{R,f}(v)
coincide with the ones for
f(v)
, that is

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}w_{R,f}(v,t)\left(\!\begin{matrix}1\\
|v|^{2}\end{matrix}\!\right)dv\!=\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v)\left(\!\begin{matrix}1\\
|v|^{2}\end{matrix}\!\right)dv\!+\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}Q(w_{R,f},w_{R,f})(v)\left(\!\begin{matrix}1\\
|v|^{2}\end{matrix}\!\right)dv\!=\!\left(\!\begin{matrix}m_{0}\\
m_{1}-m_{0}\end{matrix}\!\right),\ \forall\ R>0\,,

(4.12)

or, equivalently,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}w_{R,f}(v)-f(v)\,\left(1+|v|^{2}\right)dv=0.\quad\forall\ R>0\,.

(4.13)

Thus, in order to show
w_{R,f}(v,t)
is an element in the set (
4.7
) for
R>0
, recall first the
a priori
estimates developed for the
k^{th}
-Lebesgue moments of the collisional integral
Q(f,f)(v,t)
in Theorem
17
, after setting
s=1
, namely, for the nonlinear operator acting on
k
-Lebesgue moments,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!Q(f,f)\langle v\rangle^{2k}dv\!\leq\!{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\!-\!A_{{\bf k_{c}}}m_{1}^{-c}[f_{0}(0)]m_{k}[f]^{1+c}\!=:\!\mathcal{L}_{k}\big(m_{k}[f]\big),\ \text{for}\ c\!=\!\frac{\gamma}{2(k-1)},\ k\!\geq\!{{\bf k_{c}}}\!>\!1\,,

(4.14)

maps
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y):[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
with strictly positive constants
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
and
A_{{\bf k_{c}}}
(the coercive factor) defined in (
16
) depending on
f
only through the initial moments mass and energy initial moments
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
, respectively, the fundamental constant
\mu_{k}
from the angular averaging Lemma
12
, and the parameter
\gamma
, and the bounds of
\Phi
from (
3.10
) depending on the data, for any
k>1
.
In addition, the functional
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y)
is non-linear form function acting on
y
, that is strictly decreasing since it has a super linear negative term due to the fact that
c(k,\gamma)
is strictly positive for any
\gamma\in(0,2]
and
k>1.

As a consequence,
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y)
has
at most one positive root
characterized by
\mathcal{L}_{k}(\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*})=0
, depending on
c=\gamma/(2(k-1))
.

It is worthy to note that the root
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
coincides with the equilibrium solution associated to the flow
y_{t}(t)=\mathcal{L}y(t)
, denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{k}
defined in (
16
), to the upper ODE problem (
3.84
), with a positive initial
k
-Lebesgue moment that controls the a priori estimates for the
k
-Lebesgue moments ODE, so the root
\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}
is identify with the notation introduced in (
16
), namely

\mathfrak{h}_{k}^{*}:=\mathbb{E}_{k}=m_{1}^{\frac{c}{1+c}}\Big(\tfrac{{\mathcal{B}}_{k}}{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+c}}\,,\qquad k\geq{{\bf k_{c}}}>1\,,

(4.15)

at which
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y)
changes from positive to negative, if
y<\mathbb{E}_{k}
or
y>\mathbb{E}_{k}
respectively.

In addition, looking at the explicit formula (
4.14
),
the maximum value of the non-linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y)
is achieved at
\mathcal{L}_{k}(y)\mid_{y=0}:={\mathcal{B}}_{k}
, since
\partial_{y}\mathcal{L}_{k}(y)<0
if
c>0
, or, equivalently, if
\gamma\geq 0
, and
k>1
.

Therefore, choosing
{\bf k_{c}}
, such that
k=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}\geq{\bf k_{c}}
, not only the exponent factor becomes
c(1+\gamma)=1/2
, but also the sum of the equilibrium solution
\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}
plus the maximum value of the nonlinear operator
\max_{y\in\Omega}{\mathcal{L}}_{1+\gamma}(y):={\mathcal{B}}_{1+\gamma}
, provides a characterization to find a sufficient bound on the moment
m_{1+\gamma}[g]<{\mathfrak{h}}_{1+\gamma}\leq K,
that secures the set
\Omega
to be the invariant region, that is

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}_{1+\gamma}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}+B_{1+\gamma}=m_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big(\tfrac{{\mathcal{B}}_{1+\gamma}}{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}\Big)^{\frac{2}{3}}+{\mathcal{B}}_{1+\gamma}=m_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}\Big(\tfrac{\beta_{1+\gamma}}{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}\,\mathcal{C}_{1+\gamma}\Big)^{\frac{2}{3}}+\beta_{1+\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{1+\gamma}

(4.16)

\displaystyle=\!m_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}\left[\left(\!\frac{\beta_{1+\gamma}}{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}\!\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{1+\gamma}}{A_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}\!\right)^{1+\frac{\gamma}{3}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]+\beta_{1+\gamma}\left[\left(\!\frac{\beta_{1+\gamma}}{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}\!\right)^{2}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\frac{\beta_{1+\gamma}}{A_{{\bf k_{c}}}}\!\right)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{9}{4}\gamma}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]\leq K,

for

\displaystyle\beta_{1+\gamma}=2C_{\Phi}(2^{\frac{3}{2}\gamma}\mu_{\bf k_{c}}+1).

Now, it becomes clear that for any given
f(\cdot,t)\in\Omega
with
t
fixed, the constructed it
w_{R,f}
is an element in the intersection set defined in (
4.7
), for a choice of
\eta(\varepsilon)
that makes the limit (
4.8
) to hold, so showing that
\Omega
is an invariant region for the Boltzmann flow under consideration.

First, observe there are only two options for the associated moment
y:=m_{1+\gamma}[f]
, namely either
y
is smaller than
\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}
, or larger than it.

Thus for the first case, when
y=m_{1+\gamma}[f]\leq\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma},

and for any
\eta<1
from (
4.16
), it clearly follows that

\displaystyle m_{1+\gamma}[w_{R,f}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w_{R,f}(v)\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}dv=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(f(v)+\eta\,Q(f_{R},f_{R})(v)\big)\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}\text{d}v

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}+\eta\ \max_{y\in\Omega}{\mathcal{L}}_{1+\gamma}=\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}+\eta\ {\mathcal{B}}_{1+\gamma}\leq\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}\leq K,

(4.17)

And for the one when
y:=m_{1+\gamma}[f]>\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}
, make the choice of letting the cut-off parameter
R:=R(f)
from (
4.9
) to be sufficiently large for
m_{1+\gamma}[f_{R}]\geq\mathbb{E}_{1+\gamma}
, implying

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q(f_{R},f_{R})\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}dv\leq\mathcal{L}_{1+\gamma}\big(m_{1+\gamma}[f_{R}]\big)\leq 0\,,

and, hence,

m_{1+\gamma}[w_{R,f}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(f(v)+\eta\,Q(f_{R},f_{R})\big)\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}dv\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(v)\langle v\rangle^{2(1+\gamma)}dv\leq\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}\leq K.

(4.18)

Thus,
Part 1
is complete, since for any
f\in\Omega
, we have proven in (
4.11
) that
w_{R,f}\geq c(\eta,R)\,f(v)\geq 0
, from that the mass and energy of
w_{R,f}(v)
coincide with those of
f(v,t)
and, from (
4
) and (
4
), that
m_{1+\gamma}[w_{R,f}]\leq\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}
, and then the constructed
w_{R,f}
belongs to the invariant set
\Omega
.

Part 2:
In order to show that the constructed
w_{R,f}(v)
in
Part 1
belongs to
B(f+\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f),\eta\,\epsilon)
for the given arbitrary
\epsilon>0
, one needs to calculate the normed distance

\big\|w_{R,f}-f-\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f)\big\|_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}=\eta\,\big\|Q(f_{R},f_{R})-Q_{\gamma}(f,f)\big\|_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq 6\,\eta\,\mathfrak{h}_{1+\gamma}^{3/2}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S})}\|f_{R}-f\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\eta\,\epsilon\,,

which follows from taking the definition of
w_{R,f}-f=Q(f_{R},f_{R})
from (
4.9
) and invoking the Hölder property proven in the previous Lemma
20
, estimate (
4.6
), when taking
R\geq R(\epsilon,f)>0
, sufficiently large such that
\|f_{R}-f\|^{1/2}_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\epsilon
.

To end, adjust the choice of
w_{R,f}\in B(f+\eta\,Q[f],\eta\,\epsilon)
for
\bar{R}=\max\{R(f),R(\epsilon,f)\}
and a possible smaller
0<\eta_{*}(\epsilon,f)\leq 1/\big(C(m_{0})\big(1+\bar{R}^{\lambda}\big)\big)
to obtain, not only
w_{\bar{R},f}\in\Omega
, but also for an arbitrary
\epsilon
, it follows that

\eta^{-1}\text{dist}\big(f+\eta\,Q_{\gamma}(f,f),\Omega\big)\leq\epsilon\,,\qquad\forall\ 0<\eta\leq\eta_{*}\,.

(4.19)

Therefore taking the limit as
\eta\to 0^{+}
of (
4.19
) vanishes, proving that property (
4.8
) holds, and so the proof of Lemma
21
is now complete.

∎

The last condition to check that is satisfied is
iii)
, the One-Sided Lipschitz condition, which is a sufficient to show that if the initial
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, solutions of Theorem
7
are unique . The original proof was performaned by G. Di Blassio in
[
46
]
for the case
\gamma=1
, were polynomial moments were defined with weights
|v|^{2\gamma}
.

Lemma 22

(
one-sided Lipschitz condition – Property iii)).

For any function
f
and
g
lying in
\Omega
, it holds that

\big[f-g,Q_{\gamma}(f,f)-Q_{\gamma}(g,g)\big]_{-}\leq\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,C_{\Omega}\,\|f-g\|_{L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,

(4.20)

with
C_{\Omega}:=8K
.

Proof.

Recall that in
L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the Lipschitz bracket simply reads

\big[\varphi,\psi\big]_{-}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\psi\,\text{sign}(\varphi)\,\langle v\rangle^{2}dv\,.

(4.21)

Then, using the weak formulation (with the shorthand
\int=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}
and
h=f-g
)

\displaystyle\big[f-g

\displaystyle,Q_{\gamma}(f,f)-Q_{\gamma}(g,g)\big]_{-}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big(Q_{\gamma}(f,f)-Q_{\gamma}(g,g)\big)\,\text{sign}(f-g)\,\langle v\rangle^{2}dv

(4.22)

\displaystyle=\int\big(f\,f_{*}-g\,g_{*}\big)\Delta(v,v_{*})|u|^{\gamma}b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\text{d}\sigma\text{d}v_{*}\text{d}v=\int\big(h\,f_{*}+g\,h_{*}\big)\Delta(v,v_{*})|u|^{\gamma}b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\text{d}\sigma\text{d}v_{*}\text{d}v\,.

Here,
\Delta(v,v_{*})=\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}\,\text{sign}(h^{\prime})+\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}\,\text{sign}(h^{\prime}_{*})-\langle v\rangle^{2}\,\text{sign}(h)-\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\,\text{sign}(h_{*})
. Observe that

\displaystyle h\,\Delta(v,v_{*})

\displaystyle\leq|h|\,\Big(\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}+\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}-\langle v\rangle^{2}\Big)=2\,|h|\,\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\,,\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle h_{*}\,\Delta(v,v_{*})

\displaystyle\leq|h_{*}|\,\Big(\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}+\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}+\langle v\rangle^{2}-\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\Big)=2\,|h_{*}|\,\langle v\rangle^{2}\,.

(4.23)

Combining, both (
4.22
) and (
4
) estimates, local conservation of energy. Therefore, (with the shorthand
H=f+g
)

\displaystyle\big[f-g,

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}(f,f)-Q_{\gamma}(g,g)\big]_{-}\leq 2\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\big(|h|\,f_{*}\,\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}+|h_{*}|\,g\,\langle v\rangle^{2}\big)\,|u|^{\gamma}\,dv_{*}\,dv

(4.24)

\displaystyle=4\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|h|\,H_{*}\,\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\,|u|^{\gamma}\,dv_{*}\,dv\leq 4\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|H\|_{L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}}\|h\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}}\,.

Since
\|H\|_{L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}}=m_{1+\gamma}[f]+m_{1+\gamma}[g]\,\leq 2K
, estimate (
4.20
) follows.

∎

Hence, Lemmata
20
,
21
, and
22
ensures that conditions
\bf{i),ii),iii)}
of Theorem
19
, and so the following existence and uniqueness result, with

f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}({\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
to the initial value problem for the space homogeneous Boltzmann equation under consideration holds,

f_{t}=Q(f,f)\,,\qquad\text{with initial data}\qquad f(v,0)=f_{0}(v)\in\Omega\,,

has a unique solution
0\leq f(v,t)\in\mathcal{C}\big([0,\infty);\Omega\big)\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{1}_{2}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)\big)
.

Hence, the second stage of proof of Cauchy Problem, Theorem
7
, are fulfilled and, as a consequence, the homogeneous Boltzmann problem, and the new moment condition on the initial data can be relaxed to having an initial data that belongs to
L^{1}_{2^{+}}
for
\gamma\in(0,2]
. In fact, under Remark
11
conditions, set
\epsilon=2({\bf k_{c}}-1)\in(0,\gamma)
.

The following Lemma justifies this statement.

Lemma 23
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,2]
and
0\leq f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\epsilon\in(0,\gamma)
fixed.
Then,

i)

There exists a non negative sequence of initial data
\{f^{j}_{0}\}\subset L^{1}_{2+\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\Omega
strongly converging to
f_{0}
, that is

f^{j}_{0}\longrightarrow f_{0}\,,\quad\text{strongly in}\;L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

(4.25)

ii)

There is a sequence of solutions of the Boltzmann Cauchy problem
f^{j}(v,t)\in L^{1}_{2+\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with initial data
f^{j}_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, converging to
f\in\mathcal{C}\big([0,\infty);L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)\ \cap\ \mathcal{C}^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big),
with
2\leq\ell<2(1+\gamma),
that solves the Cauchy problem with initial data
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

Let
0\leq f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by density, there exits an approximating sequence
\{f^{j}_{0}\}\subset\Omega\subset L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, with
K
arbitrarely large, such that
\lim_{j\to\infty}f^{j}_{0}=f_{0}
in
L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying property
i)
, such that
\|f^{j}_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<C_{\epsilon}[f_{0}],
uniformly in
j
.
Such approximations can be obtained, for instance, by taking
f^{j}_{0}(v):=f_{0}(v)\,\mathbb{I}_{v<j}\in L^{1}_{\ell}
for all
\ell\leq 2(1+\gamma)
, which implies
\{f^{j}_{0}\}\subset\Omega^{j}_{\gamma}
and clearly
\|f^{j}_{0}-f_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
converges strongly to
0
.

In particular, for each
f^{j}_{0}\in\Omega_{\gamma}^{j}\subset L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the first stage of the Cauchy problem Theorem
7
secures the existence and uniqueness of solutions, invoking the tools from Lemmas
20
,
21
, and
22
which are sufficient conditions
\bf{i),ii),iii)}
for Theorem
19
to hold for each
j
fixed, that is there exists and
f^{j}(v,t)\in{\mathcal{C}}(0,\infty,\Omega^{j}_{\gamma})\ \cap\ \mathcal{C}^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big).

Next, it is important to verify the uniformity of the
\{f^{j}(\cdot,t)\}
sequence in the
L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
topology.
To this end, start from invoking the multi linearity structure of the collisional form and the one-sided Liptchitz condition (
4.24
) from Lemma
22
, which clearly remains valid independently of the pair
f^{j_{1}}
and
f^{j_{2}}
are in different
\Omega_{\gamma}^{j_{1}}
and
\Omega_{\gamma}^{j_{2}}
, respectively, to obtain
the following estimate for the difference on any pair
f^{j}
and
f^{l}
of the approximating sequence
\{f^{j}(\cdot,t)\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|f^{j}(t)-f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(f^{j}(t)-f^{l}(t))\,\text{sign}(f^{j}(t)-f^{l}(t))\langle v\rangle^{2}dv

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left(Q_{\gamma}(f^{j}+f^{l},f^{j}-f^{l})\,\text{sign}(f^{j}-f^{l})\right)(v,t)\langle v\rangle^{2}dv

(4.26)

\displaystyle\leq 4\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f^{j}(t)\!+\!f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}}\|f^{j}(t)\!-\!f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}}=:\mathcal{A}(t)\,\|f^{j}(t)\!-\!f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}},

for
\mathcal{A}(t):=4\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f^{j}(t)\!+\!f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+2\gamma}}
.

Hence, since
\|f^{j}(t)-f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}=\|f^{j}(0)-f^{l}(0)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\exp(-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{A}(\tau)d\tau)
, then, the uniformity of the sequence
\{f^{j}(\cdot,t)\}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
follows from the uniform integrability of
\mathcal{A}(t),
independently of sequence’s index
j
.

Therefore, invoking the propagation of moments and global in time estimates (
17
) from Theorem
17
for each
j
-term in the sequence, it ensures a boundedness of

\{f^{j}(t)\}
in the
L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
topology, with for all
m_{0}[f^{j}_{0}]=m_{0}[f_{0}]
and
m_{1}[f^{j}_{0}]=m_{1}[f_{0}]
, not only, for
c_{\gamma}({\bf k_{c}})=\frac{\gamma}{\epsilon}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}\|f^{j}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}}\leq\sup_{t\geq 0}m_{1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}[f^{j}](t)

\displaystyle=\max\{m_{1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}[f^{j}](0),\mathbb{E}^{j}_{c_{\gamma}(1+\epsilon)}\}

\displaystyle=:M_{1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}(m_{0}[f_{0}],m_{1}[f_{0}],b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma),\gamma,\epsilon),

(4.27)

but also, since the convergence
f^{j}_{0}\to f_{0}
in the
L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
also holds in
L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that means there is a constant
C_{\epsilon}[f^{j}_{0}]
that only depends on
m_{0}[f_{0}]
and

m_{1}[f_{0}]
, converging to the mass and energy of
f_{0}
, yielding the uniform bound estimate

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|f^{j}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}}\leq\max\big\{M_{1+\epsilon}[f_{0}],\,C_{\epsilon}[f_{0}],\gamma,\epsilon\big\}=:C_{0}(f_{0},b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma),\gamma,\epsilon),\ \ \text{ independent of}\ j.

(4.28)

Furthermore, for each fixed
j
, invoking the generation of moments and its priori global in time estimates Theorem
17
, each solution
f^{j}(t)\in{\mathcal{C}}(0,\infty,\Omega^{j}_{\gamma})\ \cap\ \mathcal{C}^{1}\big((0,\infty);L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
constructed from the initial data
f^{j}_{0}
es estimated by invoking generation global bounds (
3.81
), with
k=1+\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, so
c^{-1}_{\gamma}(k)=\frac{\gamma+\epsilon}{\gamma}
to obtain

\|f^{j}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{(2+\gamma+\epsilon)}}\leq\tilde{C}_{0}\Big(1+t^{-\frac{\gamma+\epsilon}{\gamma}}\Big)\,,\quad\text{for any}\ \ \epsilon\in(0,\gamma),

(4.29)

with constant
\tilde{C}_{0}=\tilde{C}_{0}(f_{0},b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma),\gamma,\epsilon)
independent of
j
, after combining by the aforementioned estimates (
4
) with (
4
) and (
4.28
).
Therefore, by interpolation,

\displaystyle\|f^{j}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\

\displaystyle\leq\ \|f^{j}(t)\|^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{(2+\gamma+\epsilon)}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f^{j}(t)\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\leq\ \tilde{C}_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}\left(1+t^{-\frac{\gamma+\epsilon}{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}\,\|f^{j}(t)\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\ \tilde{C}_{1}\,\mathcal{O}\!\left(1+t^{-\theta}\right),\quad\text{with }\theta=\frac{\gamma^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\in(0,1)\,,

and
\tilde{C}_{1}=\tilde{C}_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}C_{0}^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}
, uniformly in
j
.

This last estimate is crucial: not only shows the control the term in (
4
), but also allows the control of the

\|f^{j}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
by
\|f^{j}(t)\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
proportional to
\kappa(t):=\tilde{C}_{0}^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}\mathcal{O}\left(1+t^{-\frac{\gamma+\epsilon}{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}
in any time interval
t\in(0,T),\ T>0
, uniformly in the
j
-index.

Hence, the first listed fact implies the control of
\mathcal{A}(t)
in (
4
), to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(t)

\displaystyle:=4\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f^{j}(t)\!+\!f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma+\epsilon}}\leq\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\!+\!t^{-\theta}\right)\,\|f^{j}(t)\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\!+\!t^{-{\frac{\gamma^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}}}\!\right)\!,

(4.31)

where the constants
\tilde{C}_{0}
and
\tilde{C}_{1}
depend only on
f_{0},b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma),\gamma,\epsilon
, independently of the sequence’s index
j
.

Therefore, inserting this last estimate in (
4
)
leads to, after performing the time integration

\|f^{j}(t)-f^{l}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\leq\|f^{j}_{0}-f^{l}_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\exp\left(\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\!\left(t+\tfrac{\gamma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}t^{1-\theta}\right)\right)\,,\quad\text{with}\ \theta\in(0,1),\ \ 0<t\leq T.

(4.32)

As a consequence, the sequence
\{f^{j}(t)\}
is Cauchy, and so it converges strongly to a unique
0\leq f(v,t)\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T);L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, for any arbitrary
T>0
. And thus, the second estimate from (
4.30
), ensures that the limiting function
f(v,t)
satisfies for any terminal
T>0,

\|f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\ \leq\ \tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\!+\!t^{-\frac{\gamma+\epsilon}{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}\|f(t)\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{for any}\ \ \epsilon\in(0,\gamma),\ \ \ 0<t\leq T.

(4.33)

In addition, the convergence
f^{j}(v,t)\to f(v,t)
in
L^{\infty}\big([0,T);L^{1}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
holds. Invoking the interpolation estimates developed the Hölder’s estimates Lemma
20
, (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) in
L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ell\geq 2+\gamma
,
the collision operator
Q_{\gamma}(f^{j},f^{j})
is continuous operator in from
{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
into itself, shown as follows.

Indeed, recalling the previous argument from (
4
), set

\frac{d}{dt}\left(f^{j}-f^{l}\right)(v,t)=\left(Q_{\gamma}(f^{j},f^{j})-Q_{\gamma}(f^{l},f^{l})\right)(v,t)=Q_{\gamma}(H,h)(v,t),

(4.34)

for
H=f^{j}+f^{l}
and
h=f^{j}-f^{l}.

Its
L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
norm is

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q^{-}_{\gamma}(H,h)(v,t)\text{sign}(h)\langle v\rangle^{2}\text{d}v+\frac{d}{dt}\|h(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q^{+}_{\gamma}(H,h)(v,t)\text{sign}(h)\langle v\rangle^{2}\text{d}v,

(4.35)

noticing that both the gain and loss collisional forms associated
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q^{\pm}_{\gamma}(H,h)(v,t)\text{sign}(h)\,\langle v\rangle^{2}\text{d}v
are similar due to the local conservation property
\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}+\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}=\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}
. This difference of gain and loss operators are estimated by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q^{\pm}_{\gamma}(H,h)(v,t)\text{sign}(h)

\displaystyle\langle v\rangle^{2}\text{d}v\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}H_{*}\,h|u|^{\gamma}\left(\text{sign}(h^{\prime}_{*})\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2}+\text{sign}(h^{\prime})\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}\right)b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\text{d}\sigma\text{d}v_{*}\text{d}v

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Phi}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+1}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{\gamma}\right)\left(\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}+\langle v\rangle^{2}\right)|h|\,|H_{*}|\text{d}v_{*}\text{d}v

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Phi}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+2}\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|H\|^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma+\epsilon}}\,\|h\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2}}

(4.36)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Phi}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+4}\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\!+\!t^{-\theta}\right)^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}\,\|h\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2}}=:{\bf\it K}_{0}\|h\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2}},

for any
t\in(0,T)
for
T
arbitrary,
{\bf\it K}_{0}\geq C_{\Phi}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}+4}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\!+\!t^{-\theta}\right)^{\frac{\gamma-\epsilon}{\gamma}}
, is uniform a time
t
and index
j
depending only on
f_{0},b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma),\gamma,\epsilon
, for any
\gamma\in(0,2]
,
\epsilon\in(0,\gamma)
.

Therefore,
(f-f^{j})(t)=\lim_{l\to\infty}(f^{l}-f^{j})(t)
in
L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and consequently,
inserting estimates (
4
) into or (
4.34
), yields

-{\bf\it K}_{0}\,\|f^{j}-f\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2}}(t)\ \leq\ \|\frac{d}{dt}(f^{j}-f)-Q_{\gamma}(f^{j}+f,f^{j}-f)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}(t)\ <\ {\bf\it K}_{0}\,\|f^{j}-f\|^{\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma}}_{L^{1}_{2}}(t),

(4.37)

uniformly in the sequence index
j
and for all
t\in(0,T)
, for arbitrary large
T>0
.

This last estimate proves
f(v,t)\in L^{1}_{2}
is the unique solution of the Cauchy Boltzmann problem with initial data
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}
after the following regularity in time is shown.

In order to accomplish this task, replace
f^{l}
by the limiting
f
into the
identity (
4.34
) to set

\bar{\mathcal{A}}(t):=4\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f^{j}+f\|_{L^{1}_{2+\gamma}}(t)
, is the
j
-uniform upper bound from (
4.31
), yield estimates for
\bar{\mathcal{A}}(t)\leq\tilde{C}_{1}(f_{0},b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma),\gamma,\epsilon)\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\!+\!t^{-\theta}\right)

with
\theta=\left({\gamma^{2}-\epsilon^{2}}\right)/{\gamma^{2}}\in(0,1)
and
\epsilon\in(0,\gamma).

\|f-f^{j}\|_{L^{1}_{2}}(t)\leq\|f_{0}-f^{j}_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\exp\left(\tilde{C}_{1}\left(t+\tfrac{\gamma^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}t^{1-\theta}\right)\right)\,,\quad\text{with}\ \theta\in(0,1),\ \ t\geq 0.

(4.38)

To this end, using estimates developed in (
4
) for the continuity of the
L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
-norm of the collision operator, the differentiability in time follows by observing that, for any
t\geq 0.

Then, since
f(v,t)
is non-negative, both collisonal forms, gain
Q_{\gamma}^{+}(f,f)
and loss
Q_{\gamma}^{-}(f,f)
, are positive operators implying that
\|Q_{\gamma}^{\pm}(f,f)\|_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!Q_{\gamma}^{\pm}(f,f)(vat)\langle v\rangle^{2}\text{d}v
, therefore

-\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\left(1+\frac{1}{t^{\theta}}\right)\leq-\|Q_{\gamma}^{-}(f,f)\|_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\frac{d}{dt}\|f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\leq\|Q_{\gamma}^{+}(f,f)\|_{L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\tilde{C}_{1}\mathcal{O}\left(1+\frac{1}{t^{\theta}}\right).

Consequently, time integration in the interval
(s,t)
leads to

\Big|\|f(t)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}-\|f(s)\|_{L^{1}_{2}}\Big|\leq\frac{\tilde{C}_{1}}{1-\theta}\mathcal{O}\left(\left|t-s\right|^{1-\theta}\right)\,.

(4.39)

Hence, the limiting sequence
\lim_{j\to\infty}f^{j}
in
L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
makes the limiting
f(v,t)
the unique solution of the Boltzmann equation in
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}((0,\infty);L^{1}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
with initial data
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2+\epsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for any
\epsilon\in(0,\gamma).

This strong convergence of the sequence implies that the conservation laws hold as well.

Finally, using the generation of moments, estimates extends to

\Big|\frac{d}{dt}f(t)\Big|=\Big|Q_{\gamma}(f,f)(t)\Big|\in L^{1}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,\ \text{for}\ \ \ell>2,\ t>0.

The proof of Lemma
23
is complete.
∎

Consequently, the proof of Theorem
7
is also complete.

∎

Remark 16
.

The proof does not need initial bounded entropy. However, if initially so, the following estimate holds

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v,t)\ln\big(f(v,t)\big)dv\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v,0)\ln\big(f(v,0)\big)dv\,,\qquad t\geq 0\,.

5.
Propagation and generation of exponential moments.

One may view the study of exponential moments associated to a unique solutions in
f(v,t)\in L^{1}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for all
k\geq 2^{+}
, as constructed in sections
3
and
4
,
is the property that states such solution remain in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
when exponentially weighted. In fact, from probability methods it is well establish that the sumability of polynomial expectations (referred as moments in this manuscript) is related to an exponential expectation with a rate related to a few initial moments. Such observation is closely related to the use of the Fourier transform that transfer the topology of probability measure to the topology of bounded functions with the
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
norm in Fourier space. In the case of classical Boltzmann binary, elastic collisional flows, such proof is subtle, as it depends on the collision kernel, both potential and angular transition parts is in need to be verified.

In the case of the Boltzmann equation for binary elastic particle interactions, with hard sphere potentials, in three dimensions with a constant angular transitions, the first were developed in
[
26
]
, who showed that it was possible to prove that the summability of moments was true allowing to conclude that the probability density would be . These results were extended seven years later in
[
32
]
for the hard sphere case for binary inelastic collisions with bounded angular transitions, and in
[
51
]
, to show, in the classical case of binary elastic interactions for variable hard potentials, the solutions are exponential weighted in
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
if initially does as well, with a new slower rate need to be strictly positive and finite.

While much has been said about the summability of moments in the last twenty five years, the work in this manuscript clarified missing points in many of the previous approaches to this problem.
That means the extension to summability properties can now be extended to the case of hard potential rate
\gamma\in(0,2]
with a transition angular functions just satisfying integrability, as ran

This moment summability properties arise from the relation between the rate of an exponential form to its Taylor expansion in relations to and polynomial moments of the associated probability density that solves the initial valued problem to the Boltzmann flow. Hence we start by formally writing

\displaystyle E_{s}[f](t,z(t))

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v,t)\,e^{z(t)\left\langle v\right\rangle^{2s}}dv=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(t,v)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\langle v\rangle^{2sk}\frac{z^{k}(t)}{k!}dv=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{k}(t)}{k!}\,m_{sk}[f](t),\quad\text{for}\ t>0,

(5.1)

which we refer as to exponential moments with order
2s
, with
s\in(0,1]
,
s=1
corresponding to Gaussians, and rate
z(t)>0
where
z(t)
may be constant.
There are two fundamental cases to study. The first one will be the propagation of exponential moments, for which the rate
z(t)=z
is a constant function in time. The second one is the generation of exponential moments, for which the rate
z(t)=zt
for
0<t\leq 1
and
z(t)=z
is a constant function for
1\leq t
.

Such moments summability propagates whenever the initial data satisfies the same summability property, and generates when starting with
2^{+}
-moments, and invoking the moments generation results from the previous two sections, the summability of all generated moments will be finite as well.

by showing that there is an associated geometric convergent series of moments, with a positive and finite convergence radius resulting in integrability of the
L^{1}
- exponentially weighted norm, uniformly in time, whose is the rate of decay depends of the data associated to the initial flow as much as from the moments bounds in the space
L^{1}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
studied in subsection
3.35
.

In fact, these properties follows from the constructed solutions in Section
4
, which propagate or generate exponential moments depending on the integrability properties of the exponential moment of the initial data can be shown to be summable.
Propagation of initial data in this context means given
f_{0}\in\Omega
, an order factor
0<s\leq 1
and a rate
0<z_{0}
such that
E_{s}[f](0,z_{0})
is finite, then the solution of the Cauchy problem for
f(t,v)
posed in Theorem
7
, satisfies
E_{s}[f](t,z)
is finite for all time
t\geq 0
and
0<z\leq z_{0}.

However, generation of data means the following stronger property: given just
f_{0}\in\Omega
, (not necessarily
E_{s}[f](0,z_{0})
finite for any order
2s
,
0<s\leq 1
, and
0<z_{0}:=z(0)
) the solution of the corresponding Cauchy Problem,Theorem
7
, satisfies that
E_{s}[f](z(t),t)
is finite for all time
t>0
, for some order factor
2s
and rate
z(t)
to be found depending on the data.

The following Theorem proves the accuracy of these two statements. Their proofs consists in developing ordinary differential inequalities for the quantities
E_{s}[f](z(t),t)
valid for
t>0
, whose initial data is referred as to
E_{s}[f](z(t),t)\mid_{t=0}
.

The proof that follows shows the generated exponential moment, associated to such initial polynomial
k_{in}^{th}
-moment initial, has exponential order
\gamma\in(0,2]
with a rate
z
depending on the evolution variable
t
, which may be view as the ”instantaneous generation time” for a transition from a
k_{in}^{th}
-polynomial moment to an exponential tail of order
\gamma
, as well as depending on such initial data, as much as on the coercive factor, and, naturally, on the potential rate
\gamma
. This result, surely relies on the developed generation of moments estimates presented in Theorem
17
, (
3.79
) and (
16
).

The techniques developed in this manuscript, are an improvement with respect to the existing ones, in the sense that developed a unified framework for solutions of Boltzmann flows for binary elastic interactions, for hard potential and integrable differential crossections conforming the transition probabilities in such interacting particle flows.
The specifics developed here can be applied to any solution
f(v,t)\in\Omega
of the Boltzmann equation constructed by Theorem
7
, were inspired in the works on
[
26
,
32
,
51
,
8
,
74
]
for elastic and inelastic theories, by framing the summability of moments into shifted power series (or Mittag-Leffler form), as well as on the work of
[
7
]
introducing the technique of study the summability of partial sums for hard potentials with integrable angular part of the transition probability, and by non-integrable cross sections in
[
85
,
76
]
for Maxwell type of interactions with or without the angular integrability requirement.

These new techniques developed in this revision, not only are extended to Lebesgue norms in Banach Spaces, but also revised several misses on some of the cited works, but improve the characterization of calculating rather explicit exponential rates by the coercive factors, as just functions of the data. These techniques have been recently implemented
[
53
]
for a system of Boltzmann equation for disparate masses, and most recently applied to polyatomic gas models in in
[

]
.

Starting from the solution
f(v,t)\in\Omega
of the Boltzmann equation constructed by Theorem

Theorem 25

Exponential moments generation).

Remark 17
.

One should expect that for same model parameters and same initial data the exponential rate
z
are smaller for the generated exponential high energy tails than for the propagated ones. This effect may be interpreted as the generating
L^{1}
-exponential bounds from above (or coarser) than the propagating ones.

The method of proof for these two theorems presented in this manuscript is an improvement from the one inspired in the ideas
[

by the authors with collaborators by estimating the convergence of partial sums associated to (

), denoted by

yields the generation and propagation of exponential moment associated to the solution of the Boltzmann flow, characterizing their corresponding exponential order
2s
and rates

, both depend on the Cauchy problem data, on the global estimates upper bound estimates obtained for the
sk^{th}
-moments Theorem

for showing that convolutions of
probability distribution function, with bounded mass, energy, a arbitrary over the energy moment, with the potential rate function

.

Definition
.

(5.8)

In order to derive an ODI, in time

\displaystyle m_{sk}[Q_{\gamma}(f,f)]\leq\mathcal{L}_{c_{lb}}(m_{sk}[f]):={\mathcal{B}}_{sk}\!-\!\frac{\mathcal{A}_{{s}{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}{m^{-c_{\gamma}(sk+\frac{\gamma}{2})}_{1}[f_{0}]}m^{1+c_{\gamma}(sk+\frac{\gamma}{2})}_{sk}[f],\ \text{for any }\ sk\geq s{\bf k_{c}}>1,

The following form of Angular Averaging Lemma an upper forum for the collisional weight form associated to the weak formulation when testing the collision operator by the Lebesgue weight function

Lemma 26

(
Angular Averaging Lemma
\mathrm{II}
).

The Boltzmann weight function (
3.6
) associated to the weak form (
3.5
) for
\varphi(v)=\langle v\rangle^{sk}
for
sk\geq 1
,
s\in(0,1]
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, now denoted by
G_{sk}(v_{*},v)
, is estimated by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{sk}(v_{*},v)&=\int_{S^{d-1}}\Big(\langle v^{\prime}\rangle^{2sk}+\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle^{2sk}-\langle v\rangle^{2sk}-\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sk}\Big)B(|u|,\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,d\sigma\\
&\leq\Phi(|u|)\Big(\mu_{sk}\big(\langle v\rangle^{2s}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2s}\big)^{k}-\langle v\rangle^{2sk}-\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sk}\Big)\\
&\hskip-28.45274pt\leq\Phi(|u|)\bigg(\mu_{sk}\sum^{k_{j}}_{j=1}{k\choose j}\langle v\rangle^{2s(k-j)}\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sj}-(\|b\|_{L^{i}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}-\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}})\big(\langle v\rangle^{2sk}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2sk}\big)\bigg)\,,\end{split}

(5.10)

with
k_{j}=\lfloor{k+1}\rfloor
, the contractive parameter
\mu_{sk}
from (
3.26
,
3.28
) derived in the proof of the first Angular Averaging Lemma
12
.

Proof.

Applying the first Angular Averaging Lemma
12
with
j=sk
, and invoking the elementary estimate

\big(\langle v\rangle^{2}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2}\big)^{sk}\leq\big(\langle v\rangle^{2s}+\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2s}\big)^{k}\,,\quad s\in(0,1]\,,

and using the exact combinatorial binomial expansion (
3.22
), applied with
x=\langle v\rangle^{2s}
and
y=\langle v_{*}\rangle^{2s}
, yields estimate (
5.10
) for the weight function
G_{sk}(v_{*},v)
associated to the weak form of the Boltzmann flow. A detailed proof of this statement can also be found in
[
32
]
, Lemma 2.
∎

In addition, a straight forward consequence of Lemma
13

yield a sharper estimates to the collision operator’s moments, than the ones obtained in (

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}m_{sk}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}Q(f,f)(t,v)\langle v\rangle^{2sk}dv

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk}\sum^{k_{j}}_{j=1}{k\choose j}m_{sk-j}[f]m_{j+\gamma/2}[f]-(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{{\bf k_{c}}})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(v)\langle v\rangle^{2sk}(f\ast\Phi)(v)dv

(5.11)

\displaystyle:=2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk}\,S_{sk}-\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,m_{sk+\gamma/2}[f]\,.

corresponding to the
sk^{th}
-moments Ordinary Differential Inequality, where now

\displaystyle S_{sk}[f]:=\sum^{k_{j}}_{j=1}{k\choose j}m_{s(k-j)}[f]m_{sj+\gamma/2}[f]

and the coercive constant that is now is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}=c_{lb}c_{\Phi}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}})m_{0}[f_{0}],\qquad{with}\ \ b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}),

(5.13)

satisfying
2\leq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mn}}}\leq s{\bf k_{c}}\leq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}<sk
, needs to be replace by

\displaystyle\delta_{sk}:=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}{\beta_{sk}}=\frac{c_{lb}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}A_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}{\beta_{sk}}<1;\quad

\displaystyle\beta_{sk}={C}_{\Phi}(2^{(sk+\frac{\gamma}{2})}\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}}+2\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})})\leq\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\left({C}_{\Phi}2^{(sk+\frac{\gamma}{2})}+2\right),

(5.15)

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}:=\beta_{sk}\,{\mathcal{C}}^{\text{up}}_{sk}(\delta),\ \ \ \ \ \,\mathcal{C}^{\text{up}}_{sk}={\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\left[\delta_{sk}^{-e_{\alpha}}\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}+\delta_{sk}^{-e_{\theta}}\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right],

for the time invariant constant
{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]
defined in (
3.45
).

Note that
{\bf k_{lb}}
and
{\bf k_{b}}
are not ordered, since the factor
c_{lb}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}
may or may not be bigger than unity.

Therefore under these conditions the existence and uniqueness properties shown in Theorem (
19
) hold, as well as propagation and generations estimates hold, meaning that
for any initial data
M_{P}=m_{k_{\text{ini}}}[f_{i}](0)
, coercive constant
\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}=c_{lb}2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}A_{\bf k_{c}}
, following from (

),
replacing on (

) in the proof of Theorem

,
for any

yields the same results whose quantitative estimates replace

by
\mathcal{A}_{\bf k_{c}}
.

The exponents

e_{\alpha}(sk)={c^{-1}_{\gamma}(sk)}
and

, as defined in (

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}(sk)={c^{-1}_{\gamma}(sk)}=\frac{(sk-1)}{\gamma/2}>1\ \ \text{and }\ \ e_{\theta}(sk)=\frac{1}{sk-1}\left((sk-1+\gamma/2)^{2}+{\gamma/2}\right)\geq 1,

\displaystyle\frac{1+e_{\alpha}}{1+c_{\gamma}(sk)}=e_{\alpha}=\frac{2(sk-1)}{\gamma},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1+e_{\theta}}{1+c_{\gamma}(sk)}=sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}.

Next, the focus is to find from these last three inequalities a characterization for the coerciveness factor as functions of the quotient
\beta_{sk}/\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}
.

Thus, it becomes important to study the upper bounds of the a moment

\displaystyle<\!

(5.25)

In particular upper bound for both

\displaystyle m_{sk}[f](t)\leq\max\!\left\{m_{sk}[f_{0}]\,;\mathbb{E}_{c_{\gamma}{sk}}\right\}=\max\!\left\{m_{sk}[f_{0}]\,;\,\left(m_{1}\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,k}\right)^{\frac{ks-1}{sk-1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}[f_{0}]R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\right\},

hold for any

.

Hence, two fundamental estimates are need to obtain and upper ODI for the partial sums of moments of the Cauchy problem solution, set on Theorem
7
, with estimates from (
5
). One of them, estimates each partial sums coming from positive contributions of moments of the collision operator containing the term
S_{sp}
. The other one estimates from below each partial sums of negative contributions from the collision operator moments.

Such upper bound can be obtained by a weighted discrete double mixing convolution that controls partial sums of
S_{sp}
by the binary interaction of partial sums expressed by a bilinear product of the
n^{th}
-partial sum of the exponential expansion
E^{n}_{s}(t,z)
from (

), and of the
n^{th}
-partial sum of the shifted
sp+\gamma
-moment
I^{ell,n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)
, defined by

I^{n}_{s,\gamma}[f](z(t),t):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}m_{sk+\gamma/2}[f](t)\frac{z^{k}(t)}{k!}\,,

(5.32)

arises in the calculation of upper bounds for
n^{th}
-partial sums
k^{th}
-moments associated to the collision operator
Q(f,f)(v,t)
, as shown in the next Lemma.

From now on, and without loss of generality, the dependance of these partial sums on the solution
f
is omitted in the notation of moments partial sums, i.e.
E^{n}_{s}(t,z):=E^{n}_{s}[f](t,z)
and
I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z):=I^{n}_{s,\gamma}[f](t,z)
, as all estimates that follow are uniform in
f(v,t)
, for
t>0
, as it will be shown that only depends of the a finite number of initial moments associated to the initial data that solves the
Cauchy problem, Theorem
7
, by means of the moment propagation and generation estimates obtained in
Theorem
25
, as it will be indicated in the sequel

Lemma 27

(
Discrete convolution for partial sums of
k^{th}
-moments of the binary collisional form).

For any
\gamma,s\in(0,1]
and
\tilde{k}<n
, both natural numbers in
\mathbb{N}
with
s\tilde{k}\geq 1
, and
S_{sk}(t)
defined in (
5.12
), the discrete convolutional form is estimated by

\sum_{k=\tilde{k}}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}S_{sk}[f](t)\leq E^{n}_{s}(t,z)\,I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)\,,

(5.33)

where the product terms in the righthand side of this inequality have been defined in (
5.8
) and (
5.56
), respectively.

Proof.

By a simple combinatorial counting argument on the left hand side of (
5.33
), it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=\tilde{k}}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}S_{sk}[f](t)=\sum_{k=\tilde{k}}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k_{j}}{k\choose j}m_{sj+\gamma/2}[f]\,m_{s(k-j)}[f]=\sum_{k=\tilde{k}}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{j}}m_{sj+\gamma/2}[f]\frac{z^{j}}{j!}\,m_{s(k-j)}[f]\frac{z^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}

\displaystyle=\sum^{n-1}_{j=1}m_{sj+\gamma/2}[f]\frac{z^{j}}{j!}\sum_{k=\max\{\tilde{k},j\}}^{n}m_{s(k-j)}[f]\frac{z^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}\leq I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)\,E^{n}_{s}(t,z),

which proves Lemma
27
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
24
.

This proof focus on the moments summability properties from the global in time moments propagation estimates (
17
), which in fact it is enough to show it for any
t\in(0,1]
.

5.1.
Estimates for summability of moments

In oder to calculate global in time estimates for the propagation of summability of
sk
-moments for any rate
0\leq sk
.
This task is performed by controlling the partial sums
E^{n}_{s}(z,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}m_{sk}[f](t)
, for initial data
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2sk}
, with
sk\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mn}}}>2
, uniformly in time
t
and partial summability index
n
.

The way to obtain these estimates entices to invoke the estimates for of Ordinary Differential Inequalities (
3.79
) to obtain an Ordinary Differential Inequality for the
n
-sum
E^{n}_{s}(z,t)
.

Here reader needs to observe the following a delicate point regarding the following two needed properties. The first one is the propagation global
sk
-moment estimates needs to be performed under the conditions from of Theorem
16

and Theorem
17
, for any
sk\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}
.

The second one consists on the observation that estimates on an ODI for partial sum of for
E^{n}_{s}(z,t)
, needs also global upper bounds for
\frac{d}{dt}m_{sk}[f](t)=:m^{\prime}_{sk}[f](t)
for any
0\leq sk\leq s({\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1)
.

To this end, start by setting the ODI for
E^{n}_{s}(z,t)
adding up to order
0<k\leq n
the modified
sk^{th}
-moments ODIs obtained now from the
sk^{th}
-moments of the Boltzmann Equation from Angular Averaging Lemma II (Lemma
26
), and (
5.12
), to get

\displaystyle m_{sk}^{\prime}[f](t)

\displaystyle\leq\left[2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\sum^{k+1}_{j=0}{k\choose j}m_{s(k-j)}[f](t)\,m_{sj+\gamma/2}[f](t)\right]\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}+{\mathcal{B}}_{sk}\,\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}

(5.34)

\displaystyle<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}\,2^{\frac{k}{2}+1}\,m_{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\,m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}}[f_{0}]\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}+{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\,\beta_{sk}\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\,\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}.

Hence, for the first property, upper bounds for moment’s estimates for
m^{\prime}_{sk}[f](t)
,
one can obtain upper bounds for
sk^{th}
-moment derivatives, for any
1<sk\leq sk_{0}
, such that
2<s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}\leq sk_{0}
.

However, for any initial
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{2sk}
, with
sk\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}
, clearly
m_{sk}[f_{0}](t)\leq m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}}[f_{0}](t)
, for any
0\leq sk<{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}
, and so an upper bound estimate for
m^{\prime}_{sk}
is need for any
0\leq sk\leq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}
to be calculated as follows.

Therefore, after gathering all these estimates related to the coefficients and moments estimates associated to Lebesgue (or polynomial) moments, conditions are sufficient to study moments summability properties in order to find the tails associated to polynomial moments, denoted by
m_{sk}:=m_{sk}[f](t)
in the sequel.

Starting by fixing
s\in(0,1]
, summing side-by-side over
k=0,\cdots n
the sharp form estimates (
5
) of the
sk^{th}
-moment of the collision operator, the following estimate to the time variation of partial sum

, for a choice of

to be specified later, yields

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\sum_{k=0}^{n}m_{sk}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}=\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}m^{\prime}_{sk}+\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}m^{\prime}_{sk},

\displaystyle\ \ \leq\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}m^{\prime}_{sk}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}+2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\mu_{sk}S_{s,k}-\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{n}m_{sk+\gamma/2}\,\frac{z^{k}}{k!}

(5.35)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}m^{\prime}_{sk}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}+2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\mu_{sk}S_{s,k}-\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\left(I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)-\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}m_{sk+\gamma/2}\,\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\right),

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}\left(m^{\prime}_{sk}+\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}m_{sk+\gamma/2}\right)\frac{z^{k}}{k!}+2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\mu_{sk}S_{s,k}-\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)\,\frac{z^{k}}{k!},

where now

is the renormalized coercive constant, defined in (

), as the moment estimates bounds (

) are also written in terms of

.

Next we focus on estimates of each terms in the partial sum (
5.1
), for the case of propagation of initial data given by
E_{s}^{n}(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}m_{sk}[f](0)
, with all
m_{sk}[f](0)
finite, that can be estimated by means of
combining inequalities (

), (

) and (

). They are

\displaystyle m_{sk}^{\prime}

\displaystyle[f](t)<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}2^{\frac{\frac{k}{2}+3\gamma}{2}}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}\,m_{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\,m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}+{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\,\beta_{sk}\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\,\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}

\displaystyle<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}2^{\frac{\frac{{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}}{2}+3\gamma}{2}}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}\!\left[m_{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\,m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\mathbb{I}_{m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\geq\mathbb{E}_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{c(1+\frac{\gamma}{2})}\mathbb{E}_{c(s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2})}\mathbb{I}_{m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]<\mathbb{E}_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}}\right]\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}+{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\,\beta_{sk}\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\,\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}

\displaystyle<\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\left[2^{\frac{\frac{{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}}{2}+3\gamma}{2}}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}\left(m_{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\,m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]+R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{s+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\,R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\right)\right]\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\,\beta_{sk}\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk})\,\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1}

\displaystyle=:\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d\!-\!1})}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}\left(2^{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\!+\!2\!+\!2^{\frac{\frac{{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}}{2}+3\gamma}{2}}\right){\bf C}^{P}_{k}(f_{0},{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},\gamma)\,R_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\delta^{-1}_{sk}),

(5.36)

with the constant

\displaystyle{\bf C}^{P}_{k}(f_{0},{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},\gamma)\!=\!\max\left\{{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}m_{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\,m_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]\!+\!R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{s+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\,R_{\mathbb{E}}(\delta^{-1}_{s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\,;\,{\tt\bf m}_{1,sk}[f_{0}]\!\right\}.

(5.37)

Consequently, taking any
k_{0}>{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+2
, and setting
\delta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}/\beta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}
, the first term in that last RHS of (
5.1
) is estimated by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}\left(m^{\prime}_{sk}(t)+\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}m_{sk+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\right)\frac{z^{k}}{k!}

\displaystyle<\left[2^{\frac{k_{0}+3\gamma}{2}}{\bf C}^{P}_{k_{0}}(f_{0},{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},\gamma)+\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\right]\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\sum_{k=0}^{k_{0}}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}

\displaystyle<\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}\,\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\,e^{z}\ \leq\ \kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}\,\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}),

(5.38)

for the rate factor
\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})=\mathcal{R}(\beta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}/\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}})
as defined in (
5.28
),
with the propagation factor

\displaystyle\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}=\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d\!-\!1})}{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}C_{\Phi}\left(2^{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\!+\!2\!+\!2^{\frac{\frac{{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}}{2}+3\gamma}{2}}\right)\,{\bf C}^{P}_{k}(f_{0},{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},\gamma)+\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}},\

(5.39)

with the constant rate
\mathcal{R}(\beta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}/\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}})

is calculated from (
5.28
), provided that
k_{0}>{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}+1
, with
{\bf C}^{P}_{k_{0}}(f_{0},{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},\gamma)
depending on
{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}},\gamma
and on the initial
m_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f_{0}]
moments, and exponential rate
z
chosen to be

\displaystyle z\leq\ln e=1.

(5.40)

The second term from the right hand side estimate (
5.1
) is controlled by the inequality from the Discrete Convolution Lemma
5.33
and the fact that
\mu_{sk}
decreases in
sk
, to obtain

2^{\frac{k_{0}+3\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\mu_{sk_{0}}S_{s,k}\leq 2^{\frac{k_{0}+3\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk}\,E^{n}_{s}(t,z)\,I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)\,,\qquad sk_{0}\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1.

Therefore, gathering estimates (
5.1
) and (
5.1
)
the right hand side
of (
5.1
) is estimated by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}^{n}(t,z)\leq\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}\,\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})-\Big(\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}-2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk_{0}}\,E^{n}_{s}(t,z)\Big)I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)\,,\qquad sk_{0}\geq s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>1\,.

(5.41)

Next, step is motivated by the search of an exponential rate
z
small enough that enables an upper bound for
E^{n}_{s}(t,z)
. To this end, fix
z\in(0,z_{0}]
, where
z_{0}
is the exponential rate on the initial data set in (
5.2
),
and introduce the set of times

t_{n}:=\sup\big\{t\geq 0\,:\,E^{n}_{s}(\tau,z)\leq M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}],\;\forall\,\tau\in[0,t]\big\}\,,\quad\text{and}\ \ \ n\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.42)

where
M_{P}
is the initial moment with exponential tail rate
z_{0}
and the factor
(e-1)
proportional to the initial mass
m_{0}[f_{0}]
is the smallest one for any the initial exponential moment with rate
z_{0}
and order
s
, as it will become a transparent choice in the developing estimates.

Clearly, the value of
0<z_{0}
need to be chosen sufficiently small after the choice of rate (
5.40
), making
z\leq\min\{z_{0},\ln e\}\leq 1
, for each
n
fixed. Hence the partial moment sum
E^{n}_{s}(t,z)
is uniformly bounded in
t
and
n
, by showing
E^{n}_{s}(t,z)\leq M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}]
for an unbounded
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Indeed, first note that when
0<z<z_{0}
,

\displaystyle E^{n}_{s}(0,z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}m_{sk}(0)\leq E_{s}({z_{0}},0)=M_{P},\quad\text{uniformly in}\ n\in\mathbb{N},\ \text{for any}\ z\leq\min\{z_{0},\ln e\}.

(5.43)

Now, since each
m_{s}k[f]
(t) moment is continuous in time
t
, then the partial sum
E_{s}^{n}(t,z)
is also continuous in
t
. Hence, since
E_{s}^{n}(t,z)\leq M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}]
, for
t\in[0,t_{n}]
, then the right hand side of inequality (
5.41
) is dominated by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}^{n}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\!-\!\left(\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\!-\!2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk_{0}}(M_{P}\!+\!(e\!-\!1)m_{0}[f_{0}])\right)I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z),

(5.44)

for any
z\leq\min\{z_{0},\ln e\}
, with rate
\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})
from (
5.28
) and
sk_{0}>s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}>2
.

Therefore, since
0<\mu_{sk}\searrow 0
as
k\to 0
, then it is possible to choose a sufficiently large
k_{0}
, cutting-off in the initial partial sums in (
5.1
), such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}-2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}-1}C_{\Phi}\mu_{sk_{0}}(M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}])\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}{2},\ \ \text{or, equivalently,}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mu_{sk_{0}}\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}{2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}(M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}])}.

(5.45)

Remark 19
.

Note that if the angular transition
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)\in L^{p}(S^{d-1})
, for
1<p\leq\infty
, then by (
3.31
), the
k_{0}
moment order can be chosen by taking

\displaystyle sk_{0}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{2^{\frac{3\gamma}{2}}C_{\Phi}(M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}])}{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\right)^{q},\qquad\text{for}\ q=\frac{p}{p-1}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \ sk_{0}>s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}.

(5.46)

These estimates immediately lead to Ordinary Differential Inequality

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}^{n}(t,z)\leq\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\,\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}{2}\,I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)\,,\qquad\ sk_{0}>s{\bf{k}_{\text{\tiny mx}}}.

(5.47)

In addition, a lower bound for the shifted moments’ partial sum follows form a modified estimate
[
15
]
is

\displaystyle I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)

\displaystyle\geq\sum^{n}_{k=0}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\int_{\{\langle v\rangle\geq z^{-1/2}\}}f(v,t)\langle v\rangle^{2(sk+\gamma/2)}\text{d}v

\displaystyle\geq z^{-\gamma/2}\bigg(E^{n}_{s}(t,z)-\sum^{n}_{k=0}\frac{z^{k}}{k!}\int_{\{\langle v\rangle\leq z^{-1/2}\}}f(v,t)\langle v\rangle^{2sk}\text{d}v\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq z^{-\gamma/2}\bigg(E^{n}_{s}(t,z)-m_{0}[f_{0}]\sum^{n}_{k=0}\frac{z^{k(1-s)}}{k!}\bigg)>z^{-\gamma/2}\Big(E^{n}_{s}(t,z)-e^{z^{1-s}}\,m_{0}[f_{0}]\Big)

(5.48)

\displaystyle\geq z^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(E_{s}^{n}(t,z)-em_{0}[f_{0}]\right)\,.

And, since the rate is already chosen to be
0<z\leq\min\{z_{0},\ln e\},
then
1<e^{z}<e^{z^{1-s}}\leq e
in the range of
0<s\leq 1
values, as the initial data may be an arbitrary rate
z_{0}>0.

The ODI solution estimate for partial the sums
E_{s}^{n}(t,z)
is finalized after defining the state variable

X^{n}_{k_{0}}(t,z):=E_{s}^{n}(t,z)-e\,m_{0}[f_{0}],

(5.49)

whose initial condition is
X^{n}_{k_{0}}(0,z_{0}):=E_{s}^{n}(0,z_{0})-e\,m_{0}[f_{0}]=M_{P}-e\,m_{0}[f_{0}].
Therefore, writing an new ODI for
X^{n}(t,k)
after invoking the lower estimate for
I^{n}_{s,\gamma}(t,z)
from (
5.1
), applied into (
5.47
) yields

\frac{d}{dt}X^{n}_{k_{0}}(t,z)<-\tfrac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}{2z^{\gamma/2}}\,X^{n}_{k_{0}}(t,z)+\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\qquad t\in(0,t_{n})\,,

(5.50)

whose time integration of this differential inequality leads to

X^{n}_{k_{0}}(t,z)<X^{n}_{k_{0}}(0,z)\,e^{-\tfrac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,t}{2z^{\gamma/2}}}+\frac{2z^{\gamma/2}\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})}{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}\Big(1-e^{-\tfrac{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\,t}{2z^{\gamma/2}}}\Big),\qquad t\in(0,t_{n})\,.

(5.51)

Thus, the choice of rate
z>0
small enough can be made setting

z^{\gamma/2}\,\frac{2\mathcal{R}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})\,\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}}{\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}}=M_{P}-m_{0}[f_{0}]>0,\qquad t\in(0,t_{n})\,,

since
M_{P}-m_{0}[f_{0}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{z_{0}^{k}}{k!}m_{sk}(0)>0
, or equivalently, identifying a lower bound rate
z_{1}
by

z\leq z_{1}:=\,\left(\frac{{\mathcal{A}}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}/\beta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})(M_{P}-m_{0}[f_{0}])}{\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}}\right)^{2/\gamma},\qquad t\in(0,t_{n})\,,

with the rate factor
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}/\beta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}):=\mathcal{R}(\delta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})=\mathcal{R}^{-1}(\delta^{-1}_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})<1
as shown in (
5.29
), with
\beta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}
calculated from (
3.58
).

As a consequence the parameter rate
z_{1}
is uniform in
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and time
t
.

It is also worth to notice that the propagation constant
\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}
, calculated (
5.39
) is
2\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}=2^{O({\frac{k_{0}+3\gamma}{2}}+1)},

as a consequence a quantitative estimate for the
z_{1}
rate can be obtained as follows

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle:=2\left(\mathcal{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}(M_{P}-m_{0}[f_{0}])\,\frac{\mathcal{R}(\delta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})}{\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}},\qquad t\in(0,t_{n})\,,

(5.52)

with

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}(\delta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}})}{\kappa^{P}_{sk_{0}}}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\!=\!\mathcal{O}\left({2^{-\frac{k_{0}+3\gamma+2}{\gamma}}}\right)\left[\!\left(\delta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\right)^{e_{\alpha}(sk_{0})}\!\!\mathbb{I}_{k\geq{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\!+\!\left(\delta_{sk_{0}+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\right)^{\bar{e}_{\theta}(sk_{0})}\!\!\mathbb{I}_{k<{\bf k_{\gamma}}}\right]^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\!<\!1,

(5.53)

for
e_{\alpha}(sk_{0})=c^{-1}_{\gamma}(sk_{0})
and
\bar{e}_{\theta}(sk_{0})
defined in (
5.27
).
Clearly
z_{1}
degenerates the choice moment rate as
sk_{0}
grows, so the best possible rate should be taking with the smallest
sk_{0}
satisfying condition (
5.1
), or (
5.46
), depending on the integrabitlity order of the angular functions
b(\hat{u}\cdot\sigma)
.

In particular,
X^{n}_{k_{0}}(0,z)<E_{s}^{n}(0,z_{0})-m_{0}[f_{0}]
, which implies together with (
5.51
) and (
5.52
) that

E_{s}^{n}(t,z)-e\,m_{0}[f_{0}]<M_{P}-m_{0}[f_{0}]\,,\quad\text{for any}\ \ t\in(0,t_{n})\,.

with the upper bound uniform in
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,or equivalently,

\displaystyle E_{s}^{n}(t,z)<M_{P}+(e-1)m_{0}[f_{0}]\qquad\text{for all}\ t\in(0,t_{n})\ \ \text{and}\ z\leq\min\{z_{0},\ln 2,z_{1}\}<1,

(5.54)

for any
k>k_{0}
, with
k_{0}
large enough to satisfy condition (
5.1
), and a uniform in time exponential rate
z
the minimum between
z_{0}
