&\leq\|Q^{+}_{0}(f,f)\|_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|^{r-1}_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f\|^{r}_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.\end{split}

(7.25)

Taking first
R>0
and then
K>1
sufficiently large in (
7.24
) such that

2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,2^{\ell-1}\,\varepsilon(R)^{-\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,C_{\mathcal{M}}\,\ln(K)^{-1}+2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,C_{\ell,0}\,R^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{4}\,,

for example one can take

R:=2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,8\,C_{\ell,0}\,,\quad\text{and then}\quad K:=\exp\Big(\frac{2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,2^{\ell-1}\,8\,C_{\mathcal{M}}}{\varepsilon(R)^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}}\Big)\,,

(7.26)

and plugging into (
7.25
) we readily obtain, thanks to Young’s inequality, that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q(f,f)(v)\langle v\rangle^{r\,\ell}f^{r-1}(v)dv

\displaystyle\leq\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\Big(2^{\ell-1}\,2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,K^{\frac{1}{2r^{\prime}}}\,C^{1+\frac{1}{2r}}_{\ell,0}\|f\|^{r-\frac{1}{2}}_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}-\tfrac{3}{4}\|f\|^{r}_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\Big(\Big(4^{1-\frac{1}{2r}}\,2^{\ell-1}\,2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,K^{\frac{1}{2r^{\prime}}}\,C_{\ell,0}^{1+\frac{1}{2r}}\Big)^{2r}-\tfrac{1}{2}\|f\|^{r}_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)\,.

We can choose

\bar{C}_{0}:=\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\Big(4^{1-\frac{1}{2r}}\,2^{\ell-1}\,2^{\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,\varepsilon(R)^{-\frac{d}{2r^{\prime}}}\,K^{\frac{1}{2r^{\prime}}}\,C_{\ell,0}^{1+\frac{1}{2r}}\Big)^{2r}

(7.27)

which proves the result.
∎

7.2.
Propagation of
L^{r}_{\ell}
norms.

The conditions of Theorem
32
and Theorem
33
hold for any solution
f(t)
of the Boltzmann equation with finite mass and energy. Additionally, the solutions’ entropy is uniformly bounded as explained in (
7.21
) which is an important fact when addressing the case of Maxwell molecules. Thus, multiplying the Boltzmann equation (
3.8
) by
\langle v\rangle^{r\,\ell}f(v)^{r-1}
, integrating in velocity, and using Theorem
32
for the case of hard potentials, after bounding
\|f(t)\|_{L^{r}_{\frac{\gamma}{2}+{\ell}}}\geq\|f(t)\|_{L^{r}_{\ell}}
; or Theorem
33
for the case of Maxwell molecules, it follows the linear ordinary differential inequality

\frac{dX}{dt}\leq\bar{C}_{\gamma}-\tfrac{c_{lb}}{2}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,X\,,\qquad\quad\gamma\geq 0\,,\quad X(t):=\|f(t)\|^{r}_{L^{r}_{{\ell}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

(7.28)

The constant
\bar{C}_{\gamma}>0
, defined in (
7.20
) and (
7.27
), has been estimated in such theorems as functions of
C_{\ell,\gamma}
for
\gamma>0
or
C_{\ell,0}
following the definition (
7.17
).

To this end, integrating the ordinary differential inequality (
7.28
) proves the following theorem.

Theorem 34
.

Let
b\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
,
\ell\geq 0
, and
r\in(1,\infty)
. Assume that
f_{0}
satisfies that

m_{\ell_{0}}[f_{0}]<\infty\,,\quad\text{for}\quad\ell_{0}:=\max\Big\{1+\epsilon\,,\,\tfrac{\gamma+\ell}{2}\Big\}\,,

for
\epsilon>0
as small as desired, and that
\|f_{0}\|_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<+\infty
. Then, the solution
f(t)
of the Boltzmann equation (
3.8
) with initial datum
f_{0}
satisfies

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|f(t)\|_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\max\Big\{\|f_{0}\|_{L^{r}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\Big(\frac{2\,\bar{C}_{\gamma}}{c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}\Big\}\,.

The constant
\bar{C}_{\gamma}>0
is estimated in Theorem
32
equation (
7.20
) for hard potentials (
\gamma>0
) and Theorem
33
equation (
7.27
) for Maxwell molecules (
\gamma>0
) . In particular, for the case of Maxwell molecules the argument requires that the entropy must be propagated.

These constants depend on the upper bounds to solutions propagating moments of order
2\ell
for the potential rate
\alpha
, characterized in (
17
), associated to the transition probability rate of the Boltzmann flow solved in Theorem
7
, were the Lower Bound Lemma
8
calculates the positive constant
c_{lb}
, valid for solutions with
2+\epsilon
moments, and defines the coercive constant
\mathcal{A}_{\bf k_{c}}=c_{lb}2^{\gamma/2}A_{\bf k_{c}}
, with
{A}_{s{\bf k_{c}}}=2^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}c_{\Phi}(\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}-\mu_{s{\bf k_{c}}})
. In the case Maxwell type of interactions, one can simply take
c_{lb}=1
.

7.3.
Propagation of
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-norm

The main difficulty to overcome in the case
r=\infty
is the fact that the constant
\bar{C}_{\gamma}
in Theorem
34
degenerate in the limit
r\rightarrow\infty
. Indeed, recall that Riesz–Thorin interpolation played and important role in the gain of integrability result, in particular, the end-point case
(p,q,r)=(\infty,1,\infty)
. This issue is resolved using the following corollary.

Corollary 35

(
Upper bound).

Write
b=b_{S}+b_{R}
with
b_{S}
vanishing in the vicinity of
\{-1,1\}
. Further, write
b_{R}(s)=b_{R}(s)1_{\{s\geq 0\}}+b_{R}(s)1_{\{s<0\}}=:b^{+}_{R}(s)+b^{-}_{R}(s)
for the forward and backward scattering components supported in
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\pm}
respectively. Then, for any
\gamma\geq 0
it follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b_{S}}\big(g,f\big)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{b_{S}}(2,2)\|g\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,\\[3.0pt]
&Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b^{+}_{R}}\big(g,f\big)(v)\leq C\|b^{+}_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\,,\quad\text{and}\\[3.0pt]
&Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b^{-}_{R}}\big(g,f\big)(v)\leq C\|b^{-}_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\,.\end{split}

(7.29)

Proof.

The first inequality is a particular case of Theorem
28
with
(p,q,r)=(2,2,\infty)
. For the second just notice, since
b^{+}_{R}
is supported in
[0,1]
, that
|u|\leq\sqrt{2}\,|v^{\prime}_{*}-v|\leq\sqrt{2}\,\langle v^{\prime}_{*}\rangle\langle v\rangle
. Therefore,

Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b^{+}_{R}}\big(g,f\big)(v)\leq 2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}Q^{+}_{o,b^{+}_{R}}\big(g\langle\cdot\rangle^{\gamma},f\big)(v)\,\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\leq C_{b^{+}_{R}}(\infty,1)\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\,,

where we applied Theorem
28
with
(p,q,r)=(\infty,1,\infty)
in the last inequality. In addition, clearly
C_{b^{+}_{R}}(\infty,1)\sim\|b^{+}_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
. Similarly, for the term related to
b^{-}_{R}
, supported in
[-1,0)
, it follows that
|u|\leq\sqrt{2}\,|v^{\prime}-v|\leq\sqrt{2}\,\langle v^{\prime}\rangle\langle v\rangle
. Thus,

Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b^{-}_{R}}\big(g,f\big)(v)\leq 2^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}Q^{+}_{o,b^{-}_{R}}\big(g,f\langle\cdot\rangle^{\gamma}\big)(v)\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\leq C_{b^{-}_{R}}(1,\infty)\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\,.

Again, Theorem
28
with
(p,q,r)=(1,\infty,\infty)
was applied in the last inequality. The constant is estimated as
C_{b^{-}_{R}}(1,\infty)\sim\|b^{-}_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}
.
∎

A direct application of Corollary
35
gives an elementary proof of the
L^{\infty}
-norm propagation for the homogeneous Boltzmann equation. As mentioned earlier, a first proof of this fact was given in
[
20
]
under more stringent assumptions. For a discussion with addition of exponential weights refer to
[
15
]
.

Theorem 36

(
Propagation of
L^{\infty}_{\ell}
-norms).

Let
b\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
,
\gamma\in[0,1]
,
\ell\geq 0
, and assume that for
\epsilon>0
(as small as desired)

m_{\ell_{0}}[f_{0}]+\|f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=:\bar{C}_{\ell}<\infty\,,\quad\text{for}\quad\ell_{0}:=\max\Big\{1+\epsilon,{\ell+\gamma}\Big\}\,.

Then, there exists a constant
C^{\infty}_{\ell}:=C^{\infty}(\|b\|_{1},\bar{C}_{\ell},\gamma,\ell)>0
such that

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C^{\infty}_{\ell}<\infty\,,

for the solution
f(v,t)
of the Boltzmann equation.

Proof.

Let us consider the case
\ell=0
first. Due to the propagation of mass/energy for the Boltzmann equation,

\sup_{t\geq 0}m_{\gamma}[f(t)]<\infty\,.

Consequently
\|f_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|f_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{2\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\frac{1}{2}}<\infty
, and Theorem
34
applied with
r=2
and
\ell=\gamma
implies that

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty\,.

Now, Corollary
35
(with
g=f
) and Lemma
8
give us that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}

\displaystyle f(v)=Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b}(f,f)(v)-f(v)\big(f\ast|\cdot|^{\gamma}\big)(v)

(7.30)

\displaystyle=Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b_{S}}(f,f)(v)+Q^{+}_{|\cdot|^{\gamma},b_{R}}(f,f)(v)-f(v)\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,\big(f\ast|\cdot|^{\gamma}\big)(v)

\displaystyle\leq C_{b_{S}}(2,2)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+C\|b_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}-c_{lb}\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,f(v)\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\,.

Since
\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=m_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}[f]\leq m_{1}[f_{0}]<\infty
, we can choose
b_{R}>0
such that

C\|b_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f\|_{L^{1}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{c_{lb}}{2}\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}

in (
7.30
) to obtain that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(v,t)

\displaystyle\leq C(b_{S},f_{0})+\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\Big(\tfrac{c_{lb}}{2}\|f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}-c_{lb}\,f(v,t)\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\Big)\,,

(7.31)

where

C(b_{S},f_{0}):=C_{b_{S}}(2,2)\sup_{t\geq 0}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{b_{S}}(2,2)\max\Big\{\|f_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\Big(\frac{2\,\bar{C}_{\gamma}}{c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{r}}\Big\}\,.

We can integrate estimate (
7.31
) in
[0,t]
to obtain

\displaystyle f(v,t)

\displaystyle\leq f_{0}(v)e^{-c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}(t-s)}\Big(C(b_{S},f_{0})+\tfrac{c_{lb}}{2}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\|f(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}ds

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\Big(C(b_{S},f_{0})+\tfrac{c_{lb}}{2}\,\|b\|_{L^{1}}\,\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|f(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}\int^{t}_{0}e^{-c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}}\langle v\rangle^{\gamma}(t-s)}ds

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\frac{C(b_{S},f_{0})}{c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\|f(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,\quad\text{ a.e. in }v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq t\leq T\,.

The proof for this case is complete after computing the essential supremum in
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and, then, the supremum in
t\in[0,T]
. Overall,

\sup_{s\geq 0}\|f(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2\|f_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\frac{2\,C(b_{S},f_{0})}{c_{lb}\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}}\,.

Now, for the case
\ell\geq 0
notice that

Q(f,f)(v)\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\leq Q^{+}(g,g)(v)-g(v)\,\|b\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\,\big(f\ast|\cdot|^{\gamma}\big)(v)\,,\qquad g(v):=f(v)\langle v\rangle^{\ell}\,.

With this observation one can redo the previous proof with
g
instead of
f
.
∎

8.
Fine properties of the collision operator

The following computations are reminiscent of
[
25
]
. Recalling the symmetrized weak formulation of the collisional integral, that is, for a suitably regular test function
\psi(v)
, the weak form of the collision integral is given by

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q(g,f)\psi(v)dv=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g(v)f(w)B(u|,\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)\Big(\psi(v^{\prime})-\psi(v)\Big)d\sigma dwdv\,.

Let us test in particular with the classical Fourier multiplier,

\psi(v)=e^{-i\zeta\cdot v}
, where
\zeta
is the Fourier variable, we get the Fourier Transform of the collision integral through its weak form

\displaystyle\widehat{Q(g,f)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q(g,f)e^{-i\zeta\cdot v}dv

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}g(v)f(w)\frac{B(|u|,\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)}{(\sqrt{2\pi})^{d}}\Big(e^{-i\zeta\cdot v^{\prime}}-e^{-i\zeta\cdot v}\Big)d\sigma dwdv\,.

Let us use
\;\widehat{\cdot}\;
to denote the Fourier transform. It should not be a matter of confusion with unit vectors. Assuming a collision kernel
B(|u|,\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)=\Phi(u)\,b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)
, one has after trivial algebra that

\widehat{Q(g,f)}(\zeta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(v)\,f(v-u)\,\Phi(u)\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)e^{-i\zeta\cdot v}\Big(e^{-i\frac{1}{2}\zeta\cdot(|u|\sigma-u)}-1\Big)d\sigma\bigg)dudv\,.

(8.1)

Using, in the
\sigma
-integration, a reflection that interchanges
\widehat{u}
and
\widehat{\zeta}
, it follow that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)\Big(e^{-i\frac{1}{2}\zeta\cdot(|u|\sigma-u)}-1\Big)d\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}b(\widehat{\zeta}\cdot\sigma)\Big(e^{iu\cdot\zeta^{-}}-1\Big)d\sigma\,.

As a consequence,

\widehat{Q(g,f)}(\zeta)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}b(\widehat{\zeta}\cdot\sigma)\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(v)e^{-i\zeta\cdot v}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(v-u)\,\Phi(u)\Big(e^{iu\cdot\zeta^{-}}-1\Big)dudv\bigg)d\sigma\,,

which, in the case of Maxwell molecules, reduces simply to

\widehat{Q_{o}(g,f)}(\zeta)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}b(\widehat{\zeta}\cdot\sigma)\Big(\widehat{g}(\zeta^{+})\widehat{f}(\zeta^{-})-g(\zeta)f(0)\Big)d\sigma.

(8.2)

Equation (
8.2
) is regarded as Bobylev’s formula and has far reaching consequences in the fine analysis of cutoff and non-cutoff collision Boltzmann operators.

8.1.
Smoothing effects of gain operator

Assume that
b(\cdot)
is smooth and vanishes in the vicinity of
\{-1,1\}
. Additionally, assume
\Phi(u)
is smooth vanishing near the origin and at infinity. For any two
different
unitary vectors
\widehat{u},\,\widehat{\xi}
, define
\varphi
as
\cos(\varphi)=\widehat{u}\,\cdot\,\widehat{\xi}
. Here we argue in three dimension for the technical simplicity of exposition, however, these ideas can be extended to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see
[
65
,
82
]
. In particular see
[
69
]
for the Boltzmann context. In polar coordinates, we parameterize
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
as

\sigma=\cos(\theta)\widehat{u}+\sin(\theta)\sin(\phi)\widehat{w}_{1}+\sin(\theta)\cos(\phi)\widehat{w}_{2}\,.

Here we choose
\widehat{w}_{1}
in the plane generated by
\{\widehat{u},\widehat{\xi}\}
, so,
\widehat{w}_{2}
is perpendicular to both
\widehat{u}
, and
\widehat{\xi}
. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)e^{-ix\widehat{\xi}\cdot\sigma}d\sigma=\int^{\pi}_{0}b(\cos(\theta))e^{ix\cos(\theta)\sin(\varphi)}\sin(\theta)\bigg(\int^{2\pi}_{0}e^{-ix\sin(\theta)\sin(\varphi)\sin(\phi)}d\phi\bigg)d\theta\,.

The integral inside the parenthesis has stationary phase for
\phi=\{\pi/2,3\pi/2\}
. As a consequence, for
x\gg 1
it has the asymptotic behavior

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)e^{-ix\widehat{\xi}\cdot\sigma}d\sigma

\displaystyle\approx\sqrt{\frac{2}{x\,\pi}}e^{\pm i\pi/4}\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{b(\cos(\theta))}{\sqrt{\sin(\theta)\sin(\varphi)}}e^{-ix(\cos(\theta)\sin(\varphi)\pm\sin(\theta)\sin(\varphi))}\sin(\theta)d\theta

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{x\,\pi}}e^{\pm i\pi/4}\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{b(\cos(\theta))}{\sqrt{\sin(\theta)\sin(\varphi)}}e^{-ix\cos(\theta\pm\varphi)}\sin(\theta)d\theta\,.

This latter integral has stationary phase for
\theta=\{\varphi,-\varphi+\pi\}
, therefore, we conclude that

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)e^{-ix\widehat{\xi}\cdot\sigma}d\sigma\approx\frac{2}{x\pi}b(\pm\cos(\varphi))e^{\pm i\pi/4}e^{\pm ix}\,,\quad x\gg 1\,.

Now, using this asymptotic expression in formula (
8.1
) and recalling that
\Phi(\cdot)
vanishes near zero, it follows that for
|\zeta|\gg 1
,

\displaystyle\widehat{Q^{+}(g,f)}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g(v)e^{-i\zeta\cdot v/2}f(v-u)e^{-i\zeta\cdot(v-u)/2}dv\bigg)\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}b(\widehat{u}\cdot\sigma)\,\,e^{-i\frac{1}{2}(|\zeta||u|\widehat{\zeta}\cdot\sigma)}d\sigma\bigg)\Phi(u)du

\displaystyle\approx\frac{2}{\pi}e^{\pm i\pi/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g(v)e^{-i\zeta\cdot v/2}f(v-u)e^{-i\zeta\cdot(v-u)/2}dv\bigg)\frac{b(\pm\widehat{u}\cdot\widehat{\zeta})}{|\zeta||u|}\Phi(u)e^{\pm i\frac{1}{2}|\zeta||u|}du

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|\zeta|\pi}e^{\pm i\pi/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g(v)e^{-i\zeta\cdot v}\,\mathcal{P}_{osc}(\tau_{-v}\,\mathcal{R}f)(\xi)dv\,,

where
\mathcal{R}\phi(x)=\phi(-x)
is the reflexion operator and

\mathcal{P}_{osc}(\varphi)(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\varphi(u)\frac{\Phi(u)}{|u|}\,b(\pm\widehat{u}\cdot\widehat{\zeta})e^{i\frac{1}{2}(\zeta\cdot u\pm|\zeta||u|)}\,du\,.

The operator
\mathcal{P}_{osc}
is an oscillatory integral and it is a bounded operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, see
[
65
]
, as long as the phase
S(u,\xi)=u\cdot\zeta\pm|u||\zeta|
satisfies

\text{Det}\bigg(\frac{\partial^{2}S}{\partial u_{i}\partial\zeta_{j}}(u,\zeta)\bigg)=\text{Det}\big(\textbf{1}_{3\times 3}\pm\widehat{u}\,\widehat{\zeta}^{T}\big)=1\pm\widehat{u}\cdot\widehat{\zeta}\neq 0

in the support of
\Phi(u)\,b(\pm\widehat{u}\cdot\widehat{\zeta})
. The fact that
b(\cdot)
vanishes near
\{-1,1\}
ensures this condition. Thus, Minkowski’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{|\zeta|\gg 1}\big|\widehat{Q^{+}(g,f)}

\displaystyle(\zeta)|\zeta|\big|^{2}d\zeta\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\bigg(\int_{|\zeta|\gg 1}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big|\mathcal{P}_{osc}(\tau_{-v}\mathcal{R}f)(\zeta)\big|\,|g(v)|dv\Big)^{2}d\zeta\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Big(\int_{|\zeta|\gg 1}\big|\mathcal{P}_{osc}(\tau_{-v}\mathcal{R}f)(\zeta)\big|^{2}d\zeta\Big)^{1/2}\,|g(v)|dv

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\|\mathcal{P}_{osc}\|_{L^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\|\tau_{-v}\mathcal{R}f)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,|g(v)|dv\leq\frac{2}{\pi}\|\mathcal{P}_{osc}\|_{L^{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|g\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,.

We know from Theorem
28
that in the region
|\zeta|\leq R
, for any
R>0
, a similar bound holds. This leads to the estimate

\|Q^{+}(g,f)\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq C_{\Phi,b}\|g\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,,

as long as the aforementioned conditions on
\Phi
and
b
hold. This estimate was first noticed in
[
69
]
and, later, proved by generalised Radon transform methods in
[
92
,
75
]
. Such conditions can be relaxed a bit, but, such regularisation will not hold for
b\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
.

This procedure can be generalized to any dimensions and obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 37
.

Let
\Phi
and
b
be smooth with
\Phi
vanishing in a vicinity of zero and infinity, and
b
vanishing in a vicinity of
\{-1,1\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\|Q^{+}(g,f)\|_{H^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C^{1}_{\Phi,b}\|g\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,

\displaystyle\|Q^{+}(g,f)\|_{H^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}_{\Phi,b}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

We end with a remark. Since
H^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{2d}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\|Q^{+}(g,f)\|_{L^{2d}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\|Q^{+}(g,f)\|_{H^{\frac{d-1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C^{1}_{\Phi,b}\|g\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,,

which provides an interpolation point to prove an analog to Theorem
31
under more stringent assumptions in the angular kernel
b
.

Fourier methods have extensive applications in the study of fine properties of the collision operator. These methods have being used to show propagation of Sobolev norms for the homogeneous Boltzmann equation in
[
75
]
under the restriction
b\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
, based in a result of
[
23
]
. In the work
[
15
]
Fourier methods has being used in full potential to prove propagation of Sobolev regularity for general
b\in L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
.

9.
Appendix

The proof of Theorem
19
,
follows the same lines of the argument to solve ODEs in Banach spaces proposed in
[
34
]
. We include it in this manuscript for completeness. The proof is divided into three steps:

Step 1.

First note that since
\Omega
is bounded, there exists a uniform bound
K_{Q}
of
Q(u)
, for all
u
in
\Omega
.
Next, let
u
be in
\Omega
, then there exists
h_{u}>0
, such that
the intersection
B(u+hQ(u),\epsilon)\cap{\Omega}\backslash\{u+hQ(u)\}
is non-empty, for
0<h<h_{u}
and all
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small.

In addition, one can estimate
\|Q(u)-Q(v)\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}
, if
\|u-v\|\leq(K_{Q}+1)h
.
Hence, take
w
to be a point inside
B(u+hQ(u),\epsilon)\cap{\Omega}\backslash\{u+hQ(u)\}
satisfying

\|w-u-hQ(u)\|\leq\frac{\epsilon h}{4}.

Next, consider the linear map

s\mapsto\rho(s)=u+\frac{s(w-u)}{h},~~~~s\in[0,h],

and see that
\rho(s)\in\Omega
for all
s
in
[0,h]
by the convexity of
\Omega
.
Moreover, since
\dot{\rho}(s)=\frac{w-u}{h}
,

\|\dot{\rho}(s)-Q(u)\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

Now, we can see that

\|\rho(s)-u\|=\left\|\frac{s(w-u)}{h}\right\|\leq\|w-u\|\leq h\|Q(u)\|+\frac{\epsilon h}{4}<\overline{n}(K_{Q}+1)h,

which implies

\|Q({\rho}(s))-Q(u)\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4},~~\forall\,s\in[0,h].

Therefore,

\|\dot{\rho}(s)-Q({\rho}(s))\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\epsilon,~~~\forall\,s\in[0,h]\,,

(9.1)

and so,

\|\dot{\rho}(s)\|\leq 1+\,K_{Q}~~~\forall\,s\in[0,h]\ \text{and}\epsilon<1\,.

Step 2.
From Step 1, we have proved the existence of solution
\rho
to the equation (
9.1
) on an interval
[0,h]
. From this solution, we carry on the following process.

(1)

We start with the solution
\rho
, defined on
[0,h]
of (
9.1
).

(2)

Suppose such solution
\rho
of (
9.1
) is constructed on
[0,\tau]
. Since
\rho(\tau)\in\Omega
, by the same process as in Step 1, the solution
\rho
could be extended to
[\tau,\tau+h_{\tau}]
.

(3)

Next, let
\rho
be constructed by from (
9.1
) on a series of intervals
[0,\tau_{1}]
,
[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]
,
\cdots
,
[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}]
,
\cdots
. Moreover, suppose the increasing sequence
\{\tau_{n}\}
is bounded.
Then, setting

\tau=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}\,,

then, since
G({\rho})
is bounded by
C_{G}
on
[\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1}]
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},

\dot{\rho}
is bounded by
\epsilon+C_{G}
on
[0,\tau)
. Therefore, we can define
\rho(\tau)
satisfying

\rho(\tau)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\rho(\tau_{n}),\quad\dot{\rho}(\tau)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\dot{\rho}(\tau_{n}),

which implies that
\rho
is a solution of (
9.1
) on
[0,\tau]
.

By (3) of this process, we can see that if the solution
\rho
, constructed as above, is defined on
[0,T)
, it could be extended to
[0,T]
. Suppose that
[0,T]
is the maximal closed interval that
\rho
could be constructed, by Step 2 of the process,
\rho
could be extended to a larger interval
[T,T+T_{h}]
, which means that
\rho
can be constructed on the whole interval
[0,\infty)
.

Step 3.
Let us now consider two sequences of approximate solutions
u^{\epsilon}
,
w^{\epsilon}
, where
\epsilon
tends to
0
. From Step 1 and Step 2, one can see that the time interval
[0,T]
can be decomposed into

\left(\bigcup_{\gamma}I_{\gamma}\right)\bigcup\mathfrak{N},

where
I_{\gamma}
are countably many open intervals and
\mathfrak{N}
is of measure
0
.

Taking the derivative of the difference
\|u^{\epsilon}(t)-w^{\epsilon}(t)\|
gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|u^{\epsilon}(t)-w^{\epsilon}(t)\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[u^{\epsilon}-w^{\epsilon},\dot{u}^{\epsilon}(t)-\dot{w}^{\epsilon}(t)\Big]_{-}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big[u^{\epsilon}-w^{\epsilon},\dot{u}^{\epsilon}(t)-\dot{w}^{\epsilon}(t)\Big]_{-}+\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\|u^{\epsilon}(t)-w^{\epsilon}(t)\|+\epsilon,

which yields

\|u^{\epsilon}(t)-w^{\epsilon}(t)\|\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\epsilon\to 0\,,

and we have the convergence
u^{\epsilon}\to u
uniformly on
[0,T]
. The function
u
is, then, a solution of our equation. ∎
